   The oldest tag called him sun-lord; corruption of eye-of-Heaven memory; pig-
ments fugitive; keep covered. Someone later had scored through corruption and
written beneath it: precedence unestablished. A third keeper had sealed both state-
ments in place.
   The cut through corruption had bitten deep enough to score the wood beneath
the tag. The correction sat above it; the third seal held both. I rubbed a thumb
across condemnation, correction and seal, then set the tag down with all three
hands visible.
   The displaced wrapping had protected a painted board. An old man stood over
a bound boy laid on a stone, a knife raised in one hand. Behind him a ram had

                                                                                 145
Homo Noxius


caught its horns in thorns. From a break in the painted sky, a hand reached down
and closed around the man’s wrist. The order was plain: God above, father over
son, the blade between them, and the animal spared.
    My shoulder caught another panel propped behind me. I stopped it before it
fell and brought its clouded glass through the lamp-light. A man had been fixed
through hands and feet to crossed beams; his head hung forward, his ribs showed,
and blood ran from a wound in his side. Gold remained around his head. Several
figures knelt or reached toward him. One covered a face; another held the hanging
body below the wound.
    I repeated the words of the Book of the Beast silently:

       And God looked upon the Outsiders, and saw the works of
       their hands; and behold, their cruelty was greater than the
       curse that had been laid upon them. For they tortured for
       pleasure, and conquered for pride, and lied with holy words,
       and slew their children for the hunger of their fathers.

    Here a father raised the blade over his son, and worship knelt before a tortured
body opened on crossed wood. I took both as the curse made visible.
    I set the panel down too quickly and heard wood touch stone. The sound
travelled farther than I wanted. For a while I moved without raising the lamp,
passing shelves at the edge of sight: a gold mouth, a child’s clay foot, black feathers
sealed between glass, half a sentence in red. I did not stop again until the footprints
crossed my path.
    The prints ended at a shallow tray of optical pieces. Among them lay a lens
whose edge carried the shallow service cuts of Light. I knew the sequence: first
grinding, correction, approval. A foreign element had been mounted behind it in
a ring too fine for any Mountain workshop, its curvature unlike ours. I raised the
assembly toward the distant tags. Lines that had been brown blurs snapped apart.
Cracks in ink, fibres in paper and grains of dust appeared with a clarity that made
my own best work feel performed with water.
    Ka-Raedin had counted on one hand every mind in the Mountain able to fol-
low my proof. The corrective ring bore no maker’s name. I could not tell whether
it was the work of one of the Outsiders’ greatest minds or an exercise given to their
apprentices.
    The Shield label was brief: Mountain manufacture. Removed through Thresh-
old. Returned altered. Demonstration and circulation prohibited. Beneath it, in
a later hand: corrective element stable. I wanted to carry the assembly straight to
Ka-Raedin. Then I read circulation prohibited again and kept the lens in my hand.

146
                                                                     Threshold


    Through the altered lens, the high labels on the newer cabinets separated into
legible strokes. They named peoples I had never heard of: families on islands,
hunters among ice, villages under trees so tall that a whole Mountain might have
stood beneath them. Each file carried a different place and the same black tree
impressed into its cover.
    The Shield summaries repeated one outside formula: autonomy claimed; con-
tact refused; representation asserted by the Blackwood Foundation. Maps, petitions
and photographs filled the files. Some held no more than a rumour and a bound-
ary. Others had names, genealogies, inventories of weapons, diseases and men
authorised to speak. Court seals and official replies accumulated behind the pe-
titions, sometimes until the argument about a people was thicker than the record
of the people themselves.
    One thin file began with an outsider request to cut a road through a red-stone
valley. The black tree answered it. The request returned under another authority;
the tree answered again. Three changes of seal and two generations of names
passed across the pages while the refusal remained the same, until the proposed
road bent north and left the valley blank. No member of the hidden people ap-
peared anywhere in the dispute. Every official reply was addressed to Blackwood.
    I passed the file twice because nothing upon its cover named the Mountain. It
stood among all the others, neither thicker nor more guarded, and wore the same
Blackwood tree. Only when I returned to the first cabinet and read every cover
in order did I stop at it. On a festival pillar, Light hid the working mark among a
hundred marks equally bright. Here, every cover bore the tree.
    I returned the altered lens to its exact hollow after opening the file. As I
trimmed the wick, a faint metal answer travelled from the Mountain end of the
chamber. I thought of the tongue that had struck when the outer door opened. I
listened, heard nothing further, and opened another file.
    The first photograph inside showed a fair-haired woman under a hard flat light.
Beside her stood a smiling man in desert clothes, his weight divided equally over
both feet and his hands free of his robe. I knew that stillness from Shield. Behind
them rose a fold of rock I knew in my body before I knew it with my eye: the
Mountain seen from forbidden ground. Two names and a date had been copied
beneath the outsider’s marks. Sarah Blackwood. Amastan. 1974.
    The next image was all distance. A white flower towered from the horizon,
too large to be fire and too ordered to be weather; the surrounding sky had been
emptied of colour. The border carried one word and a number in the outsider
hand, repeated below in Shield script: Reggane, 1960. In the drawer beneath lay


                                                                              147
Homo Noxius


a blistered piece of green-black desert, grains melted together around bubbles,
tagged with the same place and number. Whatever had flowered in the photo-
graph had changed sand into glass.
    A map occupied the cabinet below. It showed the Mountain’s outer folds, dry
channels, wells and routes. Where the Oasis should have been, the paper held no
name or mark. Tracks curved around the blank centre. Names stopped before
reaching it and began again beyond it. Every useful line bent around the blank.
Without the photograph, I would have read it as empty ground.
    One route broke the pattern. It ran from the outcrop inward, divided be-
neath the rock and touched service ways near the Hall of Ceremony. Along it,
small boxed words repeated in several hands: Breach. Containment. Exchange. Loss.
Sealed. A newer entry carried a phrase I had never encountered in any Shield rule:
Threshold Exception.
    At the back of the bundle, a loose copy had split along its fold. Most of the
entry remained with the missing half, but the torn strip preserved three names
in Mountain hand — Sarah Blackwood, Amastan, Ka-Xhian — and below them
only Threshold Exception. It did not tell me what had been permitted, who had
requested it or what price had been paid. Ka-Xhian’s name stood beside the other
two names and above an exception to Shield’s own law.
    I folded the strip once. Taking it would be breach, and the missing strip might
expose the theft. Leaving it meant allowing the chamber to become impossible
again as soon as I stepped out. I slid it into the inner seam of my robe. Then I
returned to the first photograph. It lay loose beneath four paper corners. I lifted
them one by one and put the photograph beside the strip. The map kept its torn
edge; the empty corners kept the rectangle of the image. I had left Shield two
absences to find.
    Against the far wall lay the divided body of one machine. Two long side panels
each held a door; the rear closure had been made in an upper and lower half; a
broad front panel, painted black against the lighter body, rested above a metal
engine with eight equal throats. Upright wheels, seats, lamps, springs, a steering
wheel, a protective cage and a second fuel vessel occupied separate bays. None
of it could have passed whole through the Threshold. Someone had dismantled
it outside, carried every piece in and accounted for the machine as an anatomist
accounts for a body.
    One side panel bore a white square painted with the number 195. Near it,
beneath scratches and sun-bleaching, remained the black tree stamped on the
files. Desert sand filled the lower seam; a dry grass seed had survived behind the


148
                                                                     Threshold


window channel. Shield’s inventory dated it to the turn into 1980 and ended:
second contact; field cover concluded; body divided at Threshold; no part released.
    I was crouched beside the frame when I noticed a second lamp in the wall niche.
Its wick was dark, but the brass above it was warm beneath my fingers. Beside the
niche, a narrow tongue of metal stood proud of the wall by the width of a nail. I
had heard its twin strike when I opened the outer door.
    Stone moved somewhere behind me.
    I pinched out the entrance lamp and entered a darkness so complete that the
remembered shelves continued to stand in my sight. The dismantled machine
offered spaces without safety. I lowered myself behind two leaning panels, one
hand holding them apart to keep their edges from touching, the other pressed
against the paper inside my robe.
    A narrow line opened at the Mountain end of the chamber. A Shield warden
came through sideways and closed the stone behind her. She was a little shorter
than I. Exterior cloth widened her shoulders and covered her mouth and hair;
dust whitened her boots to the ankle. A sealed field packet was strapped beneath
one arm. She went first to the metal tongue, pressed it flush with her thumb and
waited until something answered inside the wall. Then she struck a spark into the
warm lamp I had touched moments before and lifted it. The panel against my
shoulder settled. One grain of trapped sand ran down its hollow with a sound so
slight I felt it more than heard it.
    The warden stopped. Her lamp rose. Light entered the seams around me,
found a rim, a spring, the pale underside of the steering wheel. I could see the
stitching at her wrist and, below it, Shield’s broken threshold worked black on
black. The beam rested on my hiding place long enough for the pulse in my raised
arm to move the skin.
    Then the lamp moved on. She crossed the archive, operated the outer mecha-
nism and went into the desert. The stone closed. Wind ceased. I remained behind
the panels until the arm supporting them shook hard enough to risk what fear
had not.
    I relit the entrance lamp. The flame rose smaller than before. I expected the
warden to find no one outside, study the disturbed dust and return. I could
go after her into ground I believed favoured her eyes, or take the passage from
which she had emerged while it was empty and learn where Threshold entered
the Mountain. I turned inward.




                                                                              149
Homo Noxius


    Beyond the hidden stone, a service tunnel climbed beneath the Mountain. Its
walls carried fresh scrapes at shoulder height and old oil in the hinge recesses. Far
ahead, a door bore three Shield seals, one cut over another, the oldest so deep that
the newer hands had worked around it. I did not approach it. The side opening
before it carried two office marks on the same lintel: Faith and Shield. A smell
came from it before I could decide whether to approach the sealed door.
    The archive had smelled of dry mineral and paper. From the side opening came
older air gone wrong: piss, sweat and, beneath both, the flat iron of blood. The
chamber beyond had no visible walls. Long wedges faced with pressed wool and
hair projected inward in ordered ranks, floor to ceiling; more descended from
above, hundreds of blunt points aimed into the room. At the edges, an open
metal lattice took the place of the floor. Through it I saw another field of wedges
rising from the darkness below. The room had grown teeth upon every surface
and turned all of them inward. At the centre, a square island of bare stone carried
one fixed seat, rings at the wrists and ankles, and a narrow gutter running from
between the feet to a covered drain. Black staining had entered the island’s seams
and remained after washing.
    It was the most complete silence I had known. My ears tightened as on a fast
descent. I swallowed; the sound filled my skull, and behind it came the pulse in
my neck. The floor shifted under me until I put a hand against the entrance frame.
At the foot of the seat, dried blood held one of Va-Sheva’s ribbons to the stone.
The colour and the tight thread along one edge made it hers. Had she asked the
questions there, or answered them? We had all heard stories of Shield’s private
ordeals, what it might make a warden endure before judging her worthy of being
Veil. I lifted it. Dried blood broke from the stone. I folded the ribbon once into
the inner seam of my robe and went back through the archive.


    At the outer end I returned the entrance lamp to its niche, pinched it out and
waited until the dark gave me back the wall. When I opened the stone on the
ridge’s hidden face, I could see neither the outcrop, the Mountain nor my own
hands. The warden might have been ten steps away or halfway to the horizon. I
listened until the effort made sounds of my pulse, and heard no foot on stone. A
hooded light opened beyond a low shoulder to my left, closed, then opened again
nearer the outcrop. At each opening it lit one patch of ground; between them she
moved in darkness. At its third opening the light passed across the stone above my
head. I lay against the rock until it closed. Above me, the moving light was gone.
The still star remained.

150
                                                                           Threshold


    Between two openings of the warden’s lamp, I reached the Mountain-facing
side of the ridge and sighted along the polished plate toward the shoulder of the
Mountain. I fixed that line against the still star. I still had the outward count.
Whenever the bearing wandered, I corrected it toward north. I left when the hood
closed and walked the count back. The first opening fell short. The second crossed
the ground where I had been and paused at the beginning of my prints. Whether
she had seen them I could not tell. Wind had left their centres clear. I wanted to
run. I kept the pace by which I had counted outward and put each foot on stone
where stone offered itself. When one black shoulder resembled the entrance more
closely than the one ahead, I kept the bearing.
    At the final count my hands met ordinary cold stone. I moved along the rock by
the width the vent required, found the shaved edge under my fingers and pressed
before fear could make me search elsewhere. Warmth leaked through the seam. I
opened the slab, entered and drew it shut before the searching glow reached the
opening. I reset the inner stone, brushed the disturbed dust into the older wear
and cleaned the fresh soot from my fingers.
    From far below came the last answer of the mourning, thinned by stone until
the words were gone and only the cadence remained.
    I did not go to Ka-Raedin. I believed he would summon Shield before I pos-
sessed a second witness; the strip would vanish into the chamber from which I
had taken it, and my word would stand against its record of my breach. I did not
confront Va-Sheva while I could not tell whether the ribbon accused her or asked
me to save her. Shield knew Threshold had opened. The missing ribbon would
tell Shield how far the intruder had reached; it did not yet know who had opened
Threshold, from which side, or how much had been seen. I used that uncertainty
and kept mine.
    At the first lamps, the torn strip crackled beside Va-Sheva’s folded ribbon be-
neath my robe. The photograph lay flat against both. I pressed all three to my ribs
and entered the waking passage with Sarah Blackwood’s face touching my skin.

  few breaths. I felt the last motion through the table, but I knew the last before it came.
  My legs knew it. Who was raised?
  (Sarah): — No one. The paper is from Reggane, 1960. I spent fourteen years learning
  where those marks came from, not what they meant. This morning I begin where the
  paper begins, with France manufacturing a god of its own.




154
                                                                          Reggane


    I was twenty-seven when the French lit their first bomb in the Sahara, one of
perhaps forty people in the world positioned to hear it properly. I was bored.
    France was retreating from empire and testing a weapon above land it still
called its own. A tower and device stood in the desert. The device had the harmless
name Gerboise Bleue, the blue jerboa. The briefing called its light unprecedented.
To the instruments it would be enormous and simple. I knew the broad shape it
ought to draw.
    The camp worked. Men queued with enamel mugs; a corporal spent three days
making an icebox hum without cooling; petrol found the taste of everything. Each
morning a clerk drove past with melons that split before anyone authorised their
distribution. Beyond the wire, drivers slept under lorries while mechanics rebuilt
machines from the dust inward. Forms moved between every part of it.
    At our recording station, Moussa Belkacem was paid as an instrument me-
chanic. He could level a galvanometer by listening to the table and tell from axle
dust which road a lorry had taken. He came from the Touat, had cousins along
routes the officers called empty, and corrected maps printed north of the sea with
a laugh.
    The afternoon before the test, I said the southern drums could remain idle
because there was nothing in their sector worth wasting paper on.
    “Empty? Old salt road. Two bad wells. One good well, if its owner trusts you.
Three families last month; gone now because the aeroplanes frightened their goats.
Your map arrived after they left.”
    Moussa reached past me and switched on the southern instrument.
    My husband laughed when I told him. Étienne Delorme was nine years older
than I was, a physicist, and one of those scarce men the new French state gathered
as it gathered uranium — rare, refined at great cost, and handled by men who
did not understand him and could not do without him. He owned a narrow
chapel of the device and spoke as though the cathedral had been built to keep rain
from it. He had put my name on the list for Reggane; the clearance was his doing,
remaining there mine. I was better than half the men at reading the small motions
they discarded. In public he said so. In private he checked my calculations twice,
then put his hand on my shoulder before returning them.
    Years earlier he had explained a chain reaction with dominoes set upright across
a tray: one small piece, three behind it and nine behind those, each rank taller and
heavier than the one before. He tipped the smallest. Three fell, then nine, and the
last rank struck the tray with a weight the first piece could not have supplied. “The



                                                                                155
Homo Noxius


first one supplied almost nothing,” he said. “It supplied permission,” I answered.
He kissed me.
    Edward Blackwood, my father, moved buried wealth from where it lay to
where it would sell, and the Sahara was one of his locked cellars. Yet one reach of it
defeated him. Surveys bent around it; guides altered their routes; money returned
as apology, or did not return. He pursued the blank for thirty years and died with
the loss entered under six respectable headings.
    The records occupied a real cellar beneath the Blackwood offices. Its door bore
our black wax mark: a winter tree with roots and branches made alike, so that on
a crate overturned at a port the tree still stood. I had been forbidden that room as
a child. Edward called its contents unfinished business. Six headings in his annual
accounts carried the losses.
    The domino tray stood in Paris. Edward’s survey failures lay in the cellar be-
neath the Blackwood offices. I kept them separate.
    Ground motion carried distinctions. A natural quake arrived untidily and
continued arguing with itself; an explosion entered hard through compression
and left a poorer later train. One read the whole disorder and asked what cause
could most cheaply have made it. Nature did not ordinarily wait twenty minutes,
strike again at the same weight, wait another twenty and stop at a number. Five
equal strokes at exact intervals required selection and a clock.
    On the morning of 13 February, Étienne found me checking the southern bank
as the final count came from the tower. “For once in your life, look at the thing
itself.”
    “I know what it will do.”
    “No one knows.”
    “You have spent three years telling ministers that you do.”
    One side of his mouth lifted. “Whatever you think of the men, Sarah, watch
what they have made.”
    I stood with him at the shelter opening. Eight faces turned towards the tower.
Mine was the ninth, but at the last moment I looked south. Moussa had made me
ashamed of the word nothing.
    The light passed through the dark lens, the canvas and the lids of my closed eyes.
Every seam became black; each body became an absence cut from a white world.
Though I had turned away, my shadow leapt ahead across the floor, sharp at the
shoulders and pointing south, beyond the tower and the roads on the operational
map.



156
                                                                         Reggane


    Then the ground moved beneath us with the unity of a decision. Tables walked;
glass touched metal in a hundred alarms. An auxiliary recorder reached the edge
of its stand. I caught the frame against my hip and put my left hand across the
turning drum while Moussa drove a wedge beneath its leg. The stylus crossed my
thumb, opened it and went on writing. Blood fell two finger-widths beyond the
line. I spent years avoiding the stain when I unfolded it.
    The sound came late enough to be absurd. Dust left the roof; outside, one of
the ripe melons burst, and for several seconds the greatest weapon France had
made smelled of fruit. Men cheered. Étienne watched the tower’s country rise
into itself and said, too quietly for anyone but me, “God.” Moussa was already
checking the anchors. When a soldier told him the station had held, he answered,
“Then it will not mind being checked.”
    The great signal filled the useful papers. Men marked times; cigarettes ap-
peared; the event diminished for more than an hour into reflections through layers
no one could see. I changed the bandage on my thumb and remained beside the
southern recorder.
    An hour later, the first small stroke entered beneath the tail of the great one:
a clean compression, far below the scale of the shot, arriving from the south. I
marked its time and waited. Twenty minutes is not a silence when one is watching
a line. It is long enough for a mark to seem accidental, for a man to enter, smoke
and leave, for attention to become embarrassment. At the twentieth minute the
second stroke came at the same weight. I stayed. At forty minutes the third arrived.
The fourth struck at sixty, where my pencil said it must. The fifth did the same at
eighty.
    Then nothing.
    I marked a sixth point twenty minutes ahead. At one hundred minutes, where
another clean stroke should have begun, a broad low return spread across the
instrument, as though the five small acts had gone down through the world,
touched something too large to strike, and come home wearing its breath. It lifted,
held and sank.
    Five strokes. Five waits of twenty minutes. One return.
    I checked clock, drive, stylus, anchoring bolts and paper feed, then put my fin-
gers to the housing for an engine transmitted through the table. Nothing moved
but the drum. The event had come through the ground, from beyond the test site
and the roads on the operational map. In the rough log I entered five marks and
the return, and left the source column blank. On the line above, beside the tower
shot, a clerk had written SUCCESS.


                                                                               157
Homo Noxius


    Moussa came to my shoulder before I removed the sheet.
    “Another one?”
    “The frame settling.”
    He looked from the marks to the bolts he had set. “It settles carefully.”
    “See whether they need you on the main bank.”
    He did not move. “Will this paper have my name on it?”
    “No.”
    “Then it will have yours.”
    “No.”
    He wiped blood from the drum, folded the stained corner inward so it would
not mark the next layer, and left without promising silence.
    Étienne asked that evening whether my neglected bank had produced anything
worth its paper. I said it had recorded a frame fault after the shot. He kissed the
clean knuckle beneath my dressing and said, “At least history has marked you.”
The original sheet was already inside my shirt. I slept with the first proof of the
Mountain against my ribs and my husband’s arm across the place where I had
hidden it.
    The theft measured seventy-two centimetres. Next morning the station chief
stood by the shortened roll with Moussa on one side and me on the other. I said
blood had fouled the tail and I had removed it before it contaminated the feed.
Then he asked whether the five marks in the rough log came from the damaged
frame.
    “No,” Moussa said. “The frame was true.”
    The chief looked at me, then at the personnel sheet carrying my clearance and
my husband’s unit. “It shifted under the aftershock.”
    “Not this machine,” Moussa said.
    He would not alter the maintenance slip. They moved him to the vehicle shed.
An objection from me would have reopened the missing paper, so I watched
him carry out his toolbox. At the door he set his washed handkerchief beside my
bandaged hand and asked whether I still wanted the southern bank running. I said
yes. “Then leave it running,” he said, and went.
    No charge was entered against him. His name vanished from the instrument
roster and appeared beside vehicles.
    I never saw him again. I do not know what the test put into his body or how
long he lived with it; the records do not agree. The three families he had counted
in that sector were entered in no record I have ever found, and I have looked.

                                                                             Reggane

   (Paul): — Moussa proved the frame true, not the source. The marks might still have

   (Kylian): — I can remove it now. This is the readiness sequence: the five offices make

    One event may be a fault, a vehicle, a coincidence of reflections, a clock feeding
its pulse into an amplifier. I had stolen the trace because I wanted it extraordinary.
For four years I tested every ordinary cause I could devise.
    I obtained another recorder of the same make and loosened its frame by mea-
sured amounts. I ran a generator against the wall, drove laden trucks past at dif-
ferent speeds, damaged a bearing, mistimed the feed and bled current into the
clock. Through Étienne I obtained engine schedules, blasting logs and vehicle
movements; every plausible source failed on place or time. Most dangerously,
three other stations had kept enough of the test’s tail to catch portions of my event.
One lost two strokes; another clipped the return; the third was badly levelled.
Together their bearings crossed roughly in the south and proved my recorder had
not invented the marks.
    Moussa’s maintenance slip read: Anchors checked after shot. Frame true. No
mechanical settling observed.
    Once a month I unfolded the original beneath the same lamp. I measured the
gaps, checked the ink against my copies and put it back. The paper yellowed; the
blood darkened to old tea. Repeated handling made every fibre familiar and added
no evidence.
    Étienne found me at it once in our Paris flat. I covered the five marks too late.
    “The faulty drum,” he said. “How long have you been lying to me about it?”
    “I have not lied about the instrument.”



                                                                                    159
Homo Noxius


    He drew out the opposite chair but did not sit. “That was not my question.
You always give me the sentence that is true.”
    “What do you want?”
    “The sentence next to it.”
    I gave him the technical problem without the direction, the other stations or
the paper’s origin. He listened without interrupting, then offered to obtain the
controlled-shot archive.
    The second event came in the fourth year. There had been other French shots
between and no clean repetition; that absence nearly cured me. Then Rubis was
fired underground in the Hoggar, coupling more of itself into rock. After its hard
motion crossed the stations, a southern instrument under my supervision drew
five small strokes at the old twenty-minute interval and the same broad return
twenty minutes after the fifth.
    I locked the room, took the Reggane sheet from my case and laid one trace
over the other against the window. Different instruments and different paths had
altered the lines; they did not coincide like printing. The count, twenty-minute
measure, sharp sources and slow answer survived every difference between the
drawings. I was sick into a metal bin and cleaned it before anyone could ask why.
    I returned to the window. Two different events had preserved the same delib-
erate sequence. Somewhere in the southern blank, a mechanism could put that
sequence into the ground and receive a shape through the deep Earth. It woke
rarely, after great disturbances but not all of them. I inferred that its operator
selected the disturbances it answered.
    Ka-Xhian of Shield later explained the selectivity. Reggane gave Shield its first
local nuclear signature; smaller tests could thereafter pass as known events. Rubis,
driven into rock, arrived hard enough to resemble a nearer deliberate strike whose
second blow might still be coming. Shield answered the possibility of that second
strike.
    There were also later sequences without any Outsider blast. I recorded every
one under the same cause. Kylian’s succession account in this room supplied
another: a Keeper dead, another hand in office, the five proving the transfer. The
dates in my files remained without names.
    The traces enclosed a region larger than some nations. Waves bend through
structures one has not mapped, hasten under hard rock, slow through sediment
and arrive wearing the country between. My first crossing carried an error of two
hundred kilometres.
    So I began to build ears.


160
                                                                          Reggane


    Some were scientific stations for research into the Saharan crust. Some were
commercial: Blackwood companies already searched for ore, oil and water. Some
were absurd on purpose—a study of road vibration, quarry insurance, instru-
ments in extreme heat. Each had a reason that survived inspection. After Algerian
independence, I paid for proper agreements and for useful work: several ventures
found water, two found ore, one built a road a provincial office had awaited for
nine years. The black tree appeared on salary books, water crates and geophone
cases across the desert. I kept the transparent map on which their listening arcs
met.
    No crew was asked to bend a reading. I concealed why each ear stood where it
did, and separate crews could not compare their placements.
    The first bearings were wrong. They drew us to a black volcanic ridge more
than a hundred kilometres east of the later solution: broken ground, no perma-
nent settlement, approaches visible for miles. Three parties had turned back from
it. I sent a mineral team beneath the tree and went with them.
    I refused to wait for the dust season to pass. On the sixth day, a water lorry
broke a rear spring in soft ground. As we unloaded it, a wall the colour of old
copper rose behind us. Hamid Ould Ali, the local foreman, gave us six minutes.
No one argued. We turned the lorries across the wind, cabled their wheels and
buried the instruments beneath canvas. The front reached us before the last knot.
Day became brown pressure. Sand found the scar in my thumb, though the cut
was four years healed.
    One water drum tore loose. I ran after it because it held a third of our supply.
Hamid caught my shirt and put me face-down behind a wheel. “We can lose water.
I cannot bring the director back in pieces and still be paid.” After the air cleared,
the drum stood crushed against a shelf of rock.
    In the ledger he entered one drum: wind; beneath it, director left shelter: own
decision. I said the second line was not an expense. He said it had nearly been the
largest one. The line remained.
    We spent nineteen days around the ridge, firing controlled charges at mea-
sured points. Several paths seemed to place the hidden source under its northern
shoulder. We opened an old prospect and found mineral stain and desiccated
French timber. Then a western charge reached two stations in an order impossible
through uniform ground. A buried hard body beneath the ridge bent arrivals
from farther west and placed their apparent source under its northern shoulder.
    Once measured, the distortion corrected the old bearings. The ring contracted
towards the reach my father’s ventures had refused to enter.


                                                                                161
Homo Noxius


    The corrected bearings sent me to Edward’s cellar. The executors called the seal
ceremonial. His key opened the lock; black wax still joined door to frame. I put a
chisel through the trunk of the tree.
    Inside were damp masonry, paper and the tobacco Edward had forbidden ev-
eryone else to smoke near records. Survey rolls filled pigeonholes; tin cases carried
the tree and a venture number. Nothing had moved since his last winter. Every
failed venture remained.
    For eleven days I read. Separately, each failed venture had a practical explana-
tion: a guide’s refusal, missing signatures, dust across an aerial strip. Laid beneath
the corrected bearings, the explanations formed a perimeter. Successful ventures
were precise—assays had coordinates, wages met names, a lost truck ended in a
police report. Here, routes bent before a boundary without recording an obstruc-
tion, and adjoining aerial strips disappeared in different years. Every marked route
stopped at the same ring.
    The clearest voice belonged to a Scottish geologist, Alasdair Kerr. Ordered to
cross the ring, he wrote: The guide will take us north, south, or back. He will not take
us east. I doubled the rate. He returned the money. I threatened to proceed without
him. He sat down and wished me a safe journey.
    Edward sent another guide. Kerr’s next report ended: We are not being pre-
vented. We are being allowed to decide against going. On a third attempt he de-
scribed lights without a camp, tracks crossing his own, and the same man at dif-
ferent wells who never acknowledged him. None could be separated from fatigue.
Kerr returned north, drank until Edward dismissed him and would not discuss
the country sober.
    Edward’s later notes listed guides, trading families, fuel purchases and mar-
riages between routes. Beside three failures, years apart, he had written the same
name: first with a question mark, then underlined, then hard enough to score the
sheet beneath. Amastan.
    Edward’s notes ended with that man. I wrote his name on a clean card and
began tracing his debts, kin and roads home.

    His health failed before my search narrowed to a road. He had stood near too
many devices, entered too many places after other men called them safe, and worn
his dosimeter like a watch whose time he did not intend to obey. His marrow
failed. His claim for service injury crossed six desks and returned denied. He went

physicist’s calculation at the foot of the bed.

hands could not stand the ranks, so I did.

    “I was wrong.”

three; three of the final nine fell and the rest remained standing.

    He looked at me. “You always liked the first piece.”
    “Because it released the others.”
    “Because it got to look innocent.” He stopped for breath. “It isn’t.”
    I do not know whether he meant the bomb, the doors he opened, or me. I
took the sentence for all three. I held what remained of his hand and grieved him
without reservation. When Étienne died, I signed the surviving papers with the
hand he had held. At his funeral, those permissions returned in another man’s
case.

   Martinez carried it. He did not enter the chapel. There have been two men of

appeared first on Edward’s concession payroll, then in files marked for French

channels. Most were crossed through in blue. Three were not.

   “Your father failed loudly.”

                              Confession




   I carried three things against my ribs for two days: the torn strip with its four
entries, the photograph of Sarah Blackwood beside Amastan at the outer folds,
and Va-Sheva’s ribbon stiffened with old blood.
   On the first morning I supervised two Candidates through an aperture exercise.
Their measurements differed by the width of an eyelash. I made them strip both
frames, and they found a grain of soot beneath one lower edge. They cleaned the
wood and began again.
   The taller boy finished first and rested his rule across his knees. His companion
counted the divisions again, moving his lips, then turned his slate face down. I set
the two slates together. Their measures agreed. The taller boy grinned and tapped
the other’s slate with his rule.
   The smaller boy asked to exchange frames. I sat at the end of the bench while
they checked them, their heads close together above the lower edges. They dis-
puted a division, moved the rule and began the count again. I enjoyed listening to
them. Then they laid out the frames for the next pair.
   The strip crackled inside the seam of my robe when I bent over their hands.
The ribbon lay folded beside it; the photograph lay flat beneath both.
      Sarah Blackwood. Amastan. Ka-Xhian. Threshold Exception.

   Through the first day I kept all three in separate folds of my robe. At every
lamp I considered feeding paper, photograph and ribbon to the flame together.

                                       169
Homo Noxius


Their order, material and joined presence would have gone, leaving my memory
to accuse whomever fear chose. I kept them.
   By the second evening I could make the archive establish only contact beyond
the Threshold, not its purpose. Every shelf had carried the same warning: harmless
unless taught. I had removed two records and begun supplying the lesson myself.
   I tried to make one Kahir examine another without first crossing the boundary
between them and could not. Each office alone held the facts by which its secret
acts might be judged. Justice needed an accusation before it could receive them; an
accusation needed those same facts. Whoever carried the first fact to Justice would
commit the breach that allowed Justice to ask whether a breach had occurred.
Giving Shield all three would also leave the office named by the evidence as its
only custodian. I needed the law that governed the interval before any Keeper was
accused. I slept in the robe.
   At Second Silence the next day, Ka-Raedin was teaching four senior Candi-
dates to recover a survey line after one of its floor points had shifted. Cords crossed
the chamber from three fixed lamps to a black pin set in clay. One candidate held
the last cord under tension while another read the error aloud. Ka-Raedin had let
them carry the false line almost to its consequence.
   “Stop,” I said. “Leave every cord where it stands and clear the chamber.”
   The candidate at the clay pin looked to Ka-Raedin.
   “They have one measure left,” he said.
   “My concern takes precedence.”
   “Why?”
   “Because a Veil of Light has just told you so.”
   Ka-Raedin looked from my face to the white cloth at my wrist. “Mark your
positions. Touch nothing. Outside.”
   The Candidates fixed their cords and withdrew. He waited until the last sandal
had passed the turn and for me to speak.
   “I have been thinking about Light doctrine, as you taught me to. Keeper, you
keep what must remain dark. As Veil, I am the one who places the light. What
happens if I place the light in front of something that must remain dark?”
   “I can cover it. I cannot make you, myself or others unsee what it reveals.”
   “Shield defends from the enemy without. Who defends against the enemy
within?”
   “The other four offices.”
   “What if they cannot see through the enemy’s seal?”
   “Justice can hear an accusation.”


170
                                                                       Confession


    “Not if Justice is the accused.”
    Ka-Raedin released the cord between the two pins. “What trouble have you
put yourself into?”
    “Ray this is serious, possibly existential. The Mountain may have carried a
breach before any living Keeper took office.”
    “A breach within? If it has a hand, that is treason. If it has none, defect. Which
do you mean?”
    “I cannot say.”
    “How old?”
    “I cannot say.”
    “To protect me?”
    “Partly. If Balance were emptying the stores, Justice destroying records or Faith
altering scripture, the proof would belong to the office it accused. What then?”
    “The offices keep one another by remaining divided. The office that finds the
danger preserves what it has; a second witnesses it; the offices not accused establish
standing. The office concerned answers before the others, never to itself alone.”
    “How?”
    “You have withheld the act, the accused and the proof. I cannot give you a road
without inheriting all three.”
    “Then the rule is enough.”
    Before I left, Ka-Raedin said, “Va-Kailan. Be very careful. I know of one Veil
who carried a question like yours into a sealed hearing. He did not return to his
office.”
    “Then let me consult Light’s record of it.”
    “No. That record is sealed from Veils.”
    I returned to the Veil’s Chamber before the evening lamps. I looked at the im-
age once more. Sarah Blackwood faced the hard light without narrowing her eyes.
Amastan smiled beside her. The Mountain rose behind both of them, ordinary
enough that an Outsider might have passed the photograph across a table and
seen only two people before a rock. I turned it face down.
    I laid my Light token beside the photograph. Its close rings held the old likeness
of ripples of water spreading from a point, the figure by which Ka-Raedin had led
me from the ray to interference. His refusal returned with it: the hearing record
was sealed from Veils. If I found the Keeper’s Chamber, perhaps I could reach it.

      For the Veil who follows me. If I do not return to Light, do not

                                                                       Confession


    The cradle lifted from its stem when turned against the thread of its fixing.
Beneath it lay the spare aperture leaves every Veil was taught to keep dry and
ordered. I placed the packet below them and returned the stem to the mark. I
checked the fixing twice and wiped the place my thumb had darkened. The torn
strip and ribbon remained against my ribs. I had been taught to surrender por-
tions of judgment to offices beyond my own. That surrender, more than stone,
made the Mountain possible. If I withdrew mine when its law first endangered
me, I had never believed in the order at all. I could refuse Shield’s single hand and
still submit myself to the five.
    On the third afternoon I sent Va-Sheva a route inquiry concerning a repeated
exterior reflection near an outer ventilation seam. The courtesy was permitted
between Veils. Every word in it was true. She named a narrow observation room
between Light and Shield, with a wall slit facing a sealed passage and a bench
beneath one high lamp.
    I arrived first.


   Va-Sheva entered without an escort in sight. Dark duty leather covered her
robe; her hair was bound high from her neck. She took the end of the bench
nearest the door. Her eyes counted wall slit, lamp, floor, hands and door. They
stopped at the seam of my robe.
   “You called Shield over an irregularity of light?”
   “Shield has changed you.”
   “The irregularity.”
   I gave her the answer she had given me at fifteen.
      And God called the desert the Threshold;
      and He set heat upon it by day,
      and cold upon it by night,
      and mirage upon its face,
      that no man should cross it lightly.

  “Did you mean it,” I asked, “or did you lie?”
  “What is this about?”
  “How did you become a Veil? Were you tortured?”
  “That is not for Light to know.”
  “Is that how Shield took the girl who stole lamp-oil with me and blamed the
smell on a kitchen cat?”
  “Kay. What is going on?” she asked, and then “Va-Kailan of Light, explain
yourself.”

                                                                                173
Homo Noxius


   “Is the soundless room training for capture? So an Outsider cannot make a Veil
of Shield reveal the Mountain?”
   “No Outsider has questioned me. Tell me what you saw.”
   “Before I answer, trust this much: I meant to protect the Mountain.”
   “I will remember that you said so.”
   “Promise me that if Shield takes me, I do not disappear.”
   “If Shield takes you, Balance will count you living and Justice will witness the
holding. I promise nothing beyond that.”
   “Then whatever I am called after this, remember that I came to you.”
   I drew Va-Sheva’s ribbon from my robe and held it out to her. She took it. Her
eyes found the colour, then the dried blood.
   “It was you?”
   “Yes.”
   “What else did you take?”
   “A torn record and a photograph.”
   “What did the record say?”
   “Sarah Blackwood. Amastan. Ka-Xhian. Threshold Exception. Did you know
Ka-Xhian? Was Ka-Dhavar his Veil?”
   “The succession of Shield is not yours to question. Where are they?”
   “The record is here. The photograph is beneath my seal in Light.”
   “Who did you tell?”
   “No one. Ka-Raedin heard a question of law and no fact.”
   “Give me the record.”
   I brought out the folded strip. Va-Sheva drew a dark case from her belt, laid
the ribbon inside and held the case beneath my hand. I set the strip beside it. She
closed the lid with two soft clicks.
   Va-Sheva stood, opened the door and stepped into the Shield corridor. A pol-
ished strip of stone opposite the wall slit returned the passage at an angle. In it
I saw her dark figure and the two wardens waiting beyond her. She looked at
the nearer man. Her right hand turned beside her thigh: two fingers beneath the
thumb, wrist inward, one cut towards the floor, palm opened down.
   One warden started towards the room. The other caught his arm. Both looked
at Va-Sheva through the slit as if asking whether they had read her hand correctly.
   She repeated the signal.
   They ran. Their footfalls divided at the first junction and ended.
   Va-Sheva returned to the doorway and fixed the case to her belt with a second
strap. The northern turn lay six paces beyond her shoulder. From there I could


174
                                                                       Confession


reach the Light stair without crossing a Shield door. Ka-Raedin would receive me
under my own name.
   “What did you order?”
   “What I promised. Remain here until the count reaches you.”
   I looked once at the turn and stayed where I was.
   She closed the door.


   The first buried bar fell before her steps had ended. Another answered from
below, then a chain moved behind the wall. Air pressed along the passage and the
lamp bent towards the door. The Mountain was changing state.
   When I opened the door, two Shield wardens stood at opposite ends of the
corridor. Neither approached. A Balance clerk moved between the family cham-
bers with a slate, accompanied by a Justice witness carrying a narrow black tablet.
At each door the people inside gave their number, then their names. Each voice
answered separately. Tools and water were shown. Shield sealed the latch after
Justice marked it.
   At the fifth door, one name received no answer. The mother said the boy was
asleep and began explaining too quickly that he had worked the night water turn.
The Justice witness cut the seal. Shield opened while a Faith attendant went in.
A child’s angry cry followed, then the attendant’s voice naming breath, heat and
waking. The boy answered his own name. Only then did Balance set the fifth mark
and let the count move on.
   Two Faith attendants came from the far corridor, repeating the Lesser Seal
instruction as they walked.
   “The Veil of Light is missing. Remain in your quarters for the count and until
release. Let no fear cross the threshold before the body does.”
   A Light attendant followed and dimmed three wall lamps in sequence. Each
settled into a low, stable flame. I returned to the observation room and waited.
   The latch remained free on my side. Beyond it, the Light turn was still six paces
away. Crossing those six paces would turn the act into flight and leave only Shield’s
description of it. I sat beneath the lamp with both hands visible.
   The wall niche opened when the count reached our section. Three wardens
entered through it. I stood and held out my hands. One crossed my wrists, tested
the cord and bound them beneath a dark outer cloth. Another searched the empty
seams where the strip and ribbon had been, then the belt, collar and sandal folds.
The third placed a folded gag between my teeth and fixed it behind my head. It



                                                                                175
Homo Noxius


held my jaw without closing my breath. Va-Sheva watched from the doorway with
the sealed case against her belt. She gave no order until the search was complete.
    They took me through the niche. The hidden passage was narrow and steep, its
small covered lights reaching each foot placement and no higher. Shield wardens
stood in darkness at intervals and turned aside as we passed. Through stone I heard
a family answering Balance count and a child asking whether the doors would
open before morning bread.
    I tried to mark the turns. Each doubled beneath the last; the low lamps showed
feet and withheld the walls. The cloth over my arms shifted whenever a warden
changed grip, and the four of us had to shorten step together on the steepest stair.
Once my heel missed an edge. Both wardens took my weight before the stumble
could make a sound. At one junction an old Justice witness waited, white hair
braided tight against her skull. She inspected my gag, bindings, Va-Sheva’s case
and the black line of Seal dye across her hand.
    “Body held,” the witness said.
    “Speech held,” Va-Sheva answered.
    “Proof secured?”
    “Two objects secured before Seal. One record declared beneath a Light seal.”
    “Seal witnessed.”
    They continued downward. I knew the last turn before the room appeared.
    The wedges came first: ranks of blunt points faced with pressed wool and hair,
thrust from walls and ceiling. At the edges an open metal lattice crossed above
another field of points rising from below. The central stone island held the fixed
seat, the wrist and ankle rings, the gutter and the black stain left in its seams. At
the foot of the seat, a narrow break remained in the dried blood.
    The wardens carried me over the lattice. Their footfalls ended beneath them.
Nothing returned from the wedges to tell my body where wall or ceiling stood.
The inner frame stood within a second frame, divided from the Mountain by felt
and an empty hand’s breadth. Under our joined weight the island moved by a
fraction. My body mistook the movement for a larger fall and pushed against their
arms. They placed me in the fixed seat and left the rings open. Two held position
while a third removed my bindings and gag. The first swallow after the cloth came
out sounded close enough to belong to another person. Low light reached the
island, though I could see no lamp. Behind the seat, water stood beyond a narrow
grille.
    Va-Sheva remained before me. “You are under Shield containment by Lesser
Seal. Justice has witnessed two objects in Shield custody and one record declared


176
                                                                           Confession


beneath a Light seal. Balance has entered you living. Light has received your name.
You will not be questioned until Justice summons you.”
    “You counted me.”
    “Balance did.”
    “What happens when I am summoned?”
    “Justice sets the charges.”
    “For my breach alone?”
    “For whatever has standing.”
    “I brought you a breach and committed one by bringing it. Enter both.”
    “Shield will enter yours. The other must obtain standing before the five.”
    “Then I will ask Justice for it.”
    She looked at the open rings beside my hands. “You may have made your death
more orderly.”
    “I know. I did not divide the proof so judgment would spare me.”
    “Why, then?”
    “So judgment could not stop at me.”
    Va-Sheva went to the invisible seam. At it she said, “Kay, I am so sorry.” She
turned before I could answer, and the door sealed behind her without a visible
line.
    Then the silence the room was built for closed over me. The last of her went
out of the stone, and after it came nothing: no corridor, no water, no far prayer,
no breath of the vents. Into the empty place my own body rose. The knock of
my heart came first, too loud. Then a thin sea-sound beneath it that was my own
blood. Then the dry creak of a joint as I turned my head. With no sound to set me
in the room, the floor let go of its certainty, and I put a hand to the felt to keep my
feet. I said my name once to prove that speech still existed. It returned from no
wall; it remained inside my teeth. I did not speak again. Until Justice summoned
me, I would wait here — with the knock, and the sea, and the creak, and no other
company at all.

    Amastan could not be bought. My father wrote it in a margin, in a fury, be-
cause to Edward Blackwood a man who could not be bought was a fault in the
machinery of the world. I read that margin as a daughter and as an heir. Edward
had underlined the refusal twice and scored the paper on the second stroke. Beside
it stood the rates offered, each doubled, then doubled again, each struck through.
I gave Martinez the name.
    Martinez spent the first two of those seven years putting a face and a tent to
the name. Amastan ag Malik lived among the Kel Ad, of the veiled people, the
Kel Tamasheq, whom the maps call Tuareg. The name had passed between men;
my father had dealt with more than one of them. This man moved through the
deep Tanezrouft the way I move through a drum-paper, reading a country that
looked empty to everyone else. He guided when he chose to, refused more often,

180
                                                                    Infiltration


and had turned back three of my father’s expeditions without raising his voice,
simply by declining to take them past a certain line and letting them understand
no other man would either. Martinez brought photographs from a distance and
reports bought from men who had sat at his fire. The reports named routes, kin
and wells. None named the thing he kept the routes away from.
   I gave Martinez one sentence to carry.
      A woman who read the ground wished to speak with the man
      who read the sand about a line beneath the desert that had
      moved on the morning of the French fire.

   In the fourth year he entered, under another name and with the papers of an
Oran freight broker, a transport line serving the war in northern Chad. It carried
fuel, medicine, rifles and messages south from Libya; its western carriers reached
the uranium camps around Arlit. He remained inside it for eleven months. A
driver from Agadez brought him before a man who had shared Amastan’s fire.
Two years later the same line carried Martinez to Amastan himself. They met once.
Amastan asked about Edward, Reggane, the yellow rock and why I had watched
the pen instead of the cloud. Martinez answered and came back without a route.
In the seventh year, the filing card he laid on my desk brought me to a disused
pump and a boy who watched me arrive with one driver, a canvas bag and a folded
coat. The boy left a folded paper on the pump. In a careful French hand not his
own were a place, a season and one line.
      Come without the man from Oran, and without the yellow
      rock in your mouth.

    The man from Oran was Martinez. I went.
    At the pump Martinez took my arm. He never touched anyone. Under the
noise of the engine, he told me that whatever kept the country ahead had kept it
since before Rome. It wanted no money and no talking to. He could not follow,
and would not, for anything I owned. I told him to wait there. He said a fortnight,
and no more. If I did not come back, he would put it about that I had died of the
heat, because it was tidier, and because — he said this to the pump, furious at
himself — he had gone and grown a little fond of me, against every rule of the
only trade he had. The boy drove me south. Four days later, where the track gave
out, he gave the wheel to a veiled man who had been waiting without shade. The
vehicle returned with the boy. I went on beside the veiled man, two more days on
animals, into folds the maps drew as a single brown smear. He gave no name. Only
at the seam did a voice within call him Amastan. I have not set down the route.

                                                                              181
Homo Noxius


    The Mountain, from the outside, is a tired shoulder of stone in a place there
is no reason to go. A satellite would find no gate and no wall. The ways fold into
the rock. A stranger could die of thirst within sight of a living civilisation unless
a man of the sand led him to the exact seam, set his hand against it and waited. At
the outer folds I loosened the reins. I heard the animal beneath me breathe and
the leather move under my hands. The quiet source I had followed for fourteen
years lay somewhere under the stone and made no sound at all.
    Amastan put his hand flat against a face of rock no different, that I could see,
from a hundred we had passed. A seam opened. Before I crossed, he said the one
thing of his I have kept whole.

       Thank God every day; He has been good to you.

    It was a saying of his namesake, spoken in France among the family of his friend
Charles and kept by the line after him.
    He lit an oil lamp and put it in my hand. I crossed. He closed the stone behind
me.
    The passage ran straight past three sealed crossings. At each, Shield’s mark
joined two stone leaves. Daylight lay across its far end. I entered a chamber cut
symmetrically about a low lamp-holder, with four round pillars on either side
and two stone benches facing each other. A corridor behind the farther bench
continued into the Mountain, directly opposite the one that led outside. A round
opening through the roof showed blue sky; sunlight made a disk on the floor
beside my bench.
    A man with scarred white eyes sat opposite me. Another stood behind the third
pillar on his side, perhaps forty-five, lean, with a shaven head and pale marks across
his left cheek. As I fitted my lamp into the holder, he moved far enough from the
pillar to see both my hands. I sat. The blind man waited, then spoke in English.
    “Sarah Amanda Delorme, born Blackwood, seventh of July 1932 in Green-
wich. Daughter of Sir Edward James Blackwood, born in 1894 at Greenock, and
Lady Beatrice Blackwood, born Beatrice Anne Calder in 1901 at Liverpool, sole
heir to Calder Navigation. Widow of Étienne Lucien Delorme, born in 1923 at
Nancy, physicist of the French Atomic Energy Commission, died in 1966.”
    He turned his face towards the man among the pillars. “You are in the presence
of Va-Theran, Veil of Shield under me, and of me, Ka-Xhian, Keeper of Shield.”
    “You have recited my life,” I said. “How old is this place?”
    “We were here before the Flood.”
    “What lies beneath it?”

182
                                                                    Infiltration


    “What lies beneath our temple is not spoken beyond it.”
    “Then it has already spoken. I heard it at Reggane.”
    “What did you hear?”
    “Five strokes, twenty minutes apart, after the French motion had passed. Then
a slower return through the deep rock. Rubis gave me the sequence again.”
    He questioned the intervals, the station clocks and the direction from which
each motion had arrived. I gave him the checks I had made. He asked which other
French shots had produced the sequence. I named the intervening tests and told
him that none had left a clean repetition.
    “After Reggane we knew the smaller fires,” he said. “The buried one could have
been a nearer blow, with another still to come.”
    “And at Reggane?”
    “I went to see it. Va-Theran watched the ground beyond the French lines. I
watched the fire.” He raised a finger to one ruined eyelid. “He brought me home.”
    “Who has your drawings?” Ka-Xhian asked.
    “The records are lodged with solicitors in three countries. Corrected bearings,
the Reggane trace, my father’s failures. No route. The instructions are opened if
I fail to return.”
    “When?”
    “A fortnight after I left Martinez at the pump. He will report that I died of
heat.”
    “Who follows?”
    “Whoever buys what is in the packets. A government could send a survey
before anyone settled my estate.”
    “Can you close them?”
    “By going back.”
    He asked where Martinez had stopped and what I had sent from the road. I
gave him the pump and the boy’s departure. Nothing had gone north since.
    “What did you come to decide?”
    “Whether I had found a weapon. If I have, I need to know what it defends
before I help you hide it.”
    “You will not go beneath the temple.”
    “Then show me what lives above it.”
    Ka-Xhian called his Veil forward. They spoke in a language I could not follow.
Va-Theran answered twice, then returned to the pillar for a folded cloth.




                                                                              183
Homo Noxius


    “Shield has called a drill,” Ka-Xhian said. “The wardens are exercising the
closures. Everyone else remains in quarters. Amastan, Va-Theran and I know you
are here. Keep the cloth where he puts it.”
    Va-Theran lifted the lamp from its holder and led us into the corridor behind
Ka-Xhian’s bench. At the first turn he bound the cloth over my eyes. I held his
sleeve; Ka-Xhian put a hand on his other shoulder. Va-Theran stopped us before
each step and waited while stone moved ahead of us.
    When he uncovered my eyes, we stood on a gallery above a round hall. Below
us a great disk crossed the floor on a thread whose upper fixing I could not find.
A man’s face was cut into the side towards us. I counted its crossings against my
watch. Marks stood in groups around its path, some freshly set beside the rubbed
places of earlier ones.
    Ka-Xhian told me that they kept their hours by it. He gave the drop in metres:
one hundred and twenty-five from the upper fixing to the centre of the disk. I
timed another crossing. Then he named the day’s five divisions and gave me the
interval of the Pendulum’s turning.
    We followed the gallery past closed doors to the far side of the Hall. The other
face of the disk bore a great cat with five bared teeth. At one of the doors, a row of
small sandals stood beside larger pairs. A slate rested on the lowest shelf of a rack;
beside a column of sums someone had scratched a long-eared animal. I bent to
look. Va-Theran waited until I straightened. I checked my watch before he covered
my eyes again.
    At the foot of the descent Ka-Xhian stopped us. “Shield’s arch,” Ka-Xhian
said. “The Keeper’s road. Va-Theran, take her through.” I counted five chambers
by the doors closing behind us. The cloth left a narrow view beside my nose. In the
lamplight I caught part of a toothed wheel, then a bank of pulleys with a weight
suspended between two scales. Va-Theran read out the positions. Ka-Xhian asked
him to repeat one and gave a setting in return; a chain travelled, stopped, and the
next door opened.
    Farther down, paired shafts turned at different speeds above a sliding weight.
I bent my head to follow the linkage and Va-Theran lifted my chin. In the last
chamber he had to hold a lever while the weight settled between two marks. Ka-
Xhian gave another setting. The floor trembled when the latch withdrew.
    Under the cloth I saw the bases of two pillars. Gold picked out roofs and walls
half-covered by carved sand; a narrow street continued around one of them and
disappeared beneath a dune. Va-Theran kept me moving, turned me and put a
hand between my shoulders until I knelt. He took off the cloth. I could make out


184
                                                                          Infiltration


so little beyond the lamp that I could not tell how large the chamber was. A vast
iron gate stood before me, its upper edge beyond the lamplight. The same cat’s
face I had just seen on the Pendulum was cut into its iron, five teeth bared. My
watch showed just under an hour since we had left the gallery.
    “Beyond that gate is the Beast of Hell,” Ka-Xhian said. “The five hold it in its
chains. If your people come to take it, this is what they will bring upon themselves.
Look.”
    Va-Theran turned me to the left. Across the void beside the bridge, on a wall
at right angles to the gate, a great gravure showed the Mountain above the Beast.
Five figures held its chains. Flame and water engulfed cities beyond the Mountain.
He held the lamp towards it for a full minute, then let me stand and bound the
cloth over my eyes again.


   (Sarah): — Kylian, have you been in that chamber?

You will have no access to what each keeps.”
    “I can turn a survey away. It takes time. By the time a drilling party reaches your
folds, I would have very little to work with.”

Homo Noxius


    “An instrument built for another purpose found us,” he said. “There will be

    We settled what the channel could carry. Shield would report approaching
threats through Amastan. I would act outside the folds. I could ask about an
approaching survey; I could not ask for an inner road or a means of operating
the defence. My instructions abroad would be sealed again after my return, and
Amastan would inspect the seals.
    Ka-Xhian required three oaths. I would never bring another Outsider to the
folds. I would never set the way in down in writing or in any machine that keeps a
voice. If I could no longer serve Shield’s purpose, I would send word by Amastan’s
line, withdraw, and take what I knew into silence. I said yes three times.
    “Shield has never relied on an Outsider meaning an oath,” he said. “The Excep-
tion protects itself.”
    I asked what that meant. He called Va-Theran to my bench. When I stood, stars
showed through the round hole in the roof. The lamp lit Ka-Xhian’s face from
below.
    “Keep the inner ways closed until Amastan reports her clear of the folds,” he
told his Veil.

its end he reached past my shoulder and touched the stone. It opened on Amastan,
who took me by the arm and led me outside. Va-Theran kept the lamp. Amastan
waited until the seam had closed, then took me to the animals. We camped beyond
the nearest fold and set out north after dawn.

outer fold stood one word: Horizon.

   I returned north without an account of the defence’s working. I cancelled the
release of the packets and recalled the Blackwood enquiries still directed at the
folds. Horizon began as the keeping of those three oaths.
   It began as liaison through the single quiet channel between Blackwood and
Amastan: Shield heard the outside, and I was told when I strayed too near. Then
monitoring: I gathered every stroke that empty quarter made and told myself
Shield was safer the more completely I could hear. Then management: every geol-
ogist, satellite pass and ministry rumour, each turned gently a few degrees aside.


                                                                                         187
Homo Noxius


    Turning curiosity required a body large enough to be seen doing other work.
In the six years after that stone room I took the Blackwood Foundation, already
rich and respectable, and aimed it at the work that could contain mine. It became
one of the loudest defenders in the world of peoples the century was erasing:
the uncontacted, the wandering, the ones a government calls an obstacle and a
company calls overburden. That work was real. The Foundation sent envoys, paid
lawyers, contested forced contact in courts, and broke companies off the backs
of forest and desert peoples. Some are alive today. I believe they would not be
without that work; the files can prove what we did, not the world in which we
failed to do it. It could not command a state. It could lengthen a proceeding,
expose a concession, finance the people resisting, and make an intrusion cost more
than a minister was willing to pay.
    Among its hundreds of honest files was one deliberately vague entry: a Saharan
population, unnamed and unplaced, spoken for through a desert network that
answered to Amastan and held behind a confidentiality no court ever cracked.
The file could finance resistance to an incursion after the intruder’s budget failed.
Its existence taught expeditions, ministries and ore-men to expect that resistance.
Every serious question about an isolated people entered a Blackwood office and
climbed a chain of clerks. Anything touching one quarter of the Tanezrouft
reached the last desk. I sat at it.
    The other files could not be dummies: any hostile audit that opened one hol-
low protection would put a finger through the Saharan one. Each people therefore
had counsel, field money, witnesses and a board prepared to spend in public.
When a government waited or a company returned under another name, the case
remained funded and the same clerks were still there. Some protections outlived
the concealment that had first made me choose them. I signed those renewals too.


   (Kylian): — … You hid it the way Light hides the mirror in the great pillar. Not by
   keeping the one sign secret: by raising a hundred true signs around it, every one of

   Horizon kept files on armies, ministries, ore-men, surveys and satellite passes.
The motor race lay under amusement. It was young then, and poor, Paris to Dakar
across the width of the Sahara, run by amateurs for the love of nearly dying. Its first


188
                                                                    Infiltration


running had gone from Reggane away east toward In Salah. In the late autumn of
nineteen seventy-nine the next route was published. I read it standing up: on the
eighth of January more than two hundred machines would leave Reggane and
drive due south, toward Bordj Badji Mokhtar, along a line that passed within a
lost man’s wandering of the outer folds.
    I went to the Mountain a second time, and it was the last time I saw the blind
man. I brought him the route and told him what I had already done, because there
was no time left to ask. The Foundation had made itself, in a season, a patron
of the race: medicine, recovery vehicles, liaison with the desert peoples the route
would cross. Inside that apparatus I had entered drivers as gentleman amateurs
and placed my people in the assistance crews, sweep vehicles and radio posts. “We
watched governments, geologists and soldiers,” I told him. “None of us watched a
race. The machines that hold the line are no danger. The lost ones are. Blackwood
will be ahead of them, among them and behind them. On your side, set the fullest
seal you know for the days it takes them to pass.” He said that Shield did not
require an Outsider to teach it how to shut a door. Then he set the seal. I never
spoke with Ka-Xhian again.
    On the eighth of January I sat in the Foundation recovery post with three radio
sets and the route pinned to a canvas wall. Two wanderers accepted fuel and a
bearing back to the marked line. The third did not answer. A spotter south of
Reggane reported one machine moving alone in bad light, its compass taking it
towards the outer folds. He gave the bearing twice, then left the channel clear as
he had been trained.
    “White Six,” I said into the Foundation set. “There is a competitor east of your
line. Find him. Do not signal race control unless he refuses you.”
    White Six was a sand-coloured motor Blackwood had entered, our small crest
on its door beneath the race number. It found the lost driver less than an hour
from the folds. He mistook it for another competitor, accepted the line it offered
and followed when it cut back west. The two machines appeared together at the
recovery post before dark. The lost one continued south. White Six arrived with
its steering box cracked, the left front suspension driven through its mount, and
the driver holding the wheel a quarter-turn against a pull that would have killed
him at speed.
    The retirement entered the public record as a steering failure. After the race
moved south, my mechanics took White Six back towards the folds and disman-
tled it where the vehicle could go no farther. Shield took delivery there, load by
load. Amastan reported that its pieces had been stored in the Veil’s Chamber. He


                                                                               189
Homo Noxius


also reported that the great closures at Reggane and during the race had both been
presented as drills.
    The race work left true papers behind it: fuel dockets, medical logs, one water
reconnaissance run under the recovery flag. Its cores went sealed into a Blackwood
store, and the catalogue still holds them. One hole west of the marked line gave
sweet water and an assay heavy in lithium the laboratory queried twice. Nothing
of it was ever published.
    Then routes: ways in and out, cached and tested. Then mapping, with listening
stations placed around the Mountain. I extended their coverage knock by knock
and kept each trace with the earlier ones. By then Horizon could warn me of a
traveller at the outer folds, remove him from the approach, or place another man
there in his stead.
    There was a survey, a consortium of three companies and two governments,
that came within a season of running a deep line across the folds for further oil
exploration. I did not bribe them. Across two years and eleven hands I let their
funding develop a doubt, sent their lead geologist a flattering offer in Western
Australia, and tangled their permits in a border dispute I helped to warm. Every
piece was true, or true enough to survive a check; none could be traced within a
thousand miles of a woman in Switzerland. The survey folded. Its people never
knew Horizon had made the decision for them.
