                                   Chapter 14



                                     Trial




    Proof secured. Speech held. Body held. Justice had named those three facts over
me while I was still bound; they were the last words I could place in time. Inside
the soundless room I had no way to count the hours.
    Near First Light the door opened, and Ka-Dhavar of Shield stood in it. “You
will not be bound,” he said. His voice struck the inside of my head; for one breath
I understood the sound before the words. “The Lesser Seal remains. The count is
still looking for you.” “There is nowhere in the Mountain a man runs to,” I said,
“and no man runs from the five.” He stepped aside to let me pass.
    He took me past every hearing room I knew, and the route descended. The
household doors were barred. At two junctions a Shield warden stood aside to let
us through; we met no one else. The warmth of habitation thinned and the polish
left the stone.
    Then white stone, and the Hall of Prayers. The sight of them assembled
stopped me on the threshold. Ka-Elun of Faith at the centre in her plain white
and the torn pilgrim’s mantle. Ka-Cheryn of Balance, broad and grey. Ka-Leth of
Justice, still as his own tablet. Ka-Raedin, my Keeper, a hooded hand-lamp in his
grip. And apart from the five, only two: Va-Sheva of Shield, who looked past me,
and Sa-Tavan of Justice, a Candidate still, too junior by years to stand in such a
room. At their feet were packs: water, dried food, folded cloth.
    Ka-Elun looked at the lamp in Ka-Raedin’s hand. “Keeper of Light,” she said
softly, “put that away.” He held still. “It is hooded.” “That is not the same as away.

                                        193
Homo Noxius


Better Keepers of Light than either of you have entered the Labyrinth believing
brightness would make a path obedient. They were recovered by pilgrims who
could not read a mirror but could keep breath with stone.” Ka-Raedin held the
lamp through one full breath. Then he set it against the wall, hooded and unlit.
“Faith receives,” Ka-Elun said, and let it lie.
    “You will walk,” Ka-Elun told me.
    “Guided by whom?”
    “By what remains when sight stops governing…”
    The carved prayer moved inward, opening a passage behind Ka-Elun. She took
a narrow lamp from the bracket beside it. We went down in single file, Ka-Dhavar
last, closing the stone behind us. At the first division Ka-Elun sang one low note
and waited. The passage on our left returned it at once; the one on our right gave
it back later and fainter. She took the right.
    We walked for an hour — ramp and switchback and long shallow grade, dou-
bling back on itself, descending until my ears changed and the air cooled against
my face. At the next branch she used a child’s counting tune; at the next, four
words from the prayer at First Light. She waited through every return before
choosing.
    “Are we going to the Hall of the Last Prayer?” I asked. Ka-Elun came close to
laughing. “No. You are being taken beneath the rooms, Va-Kailan — beneath the
reason for them…”
    The walls were striped, floor to vanished ceiling, bands of pale and dark lit by
Ka-Elun’s lamp so that stone and shadow repeated in one endless rhythm. We
turned left, right, then doubled back through a low chamber whose floor sloped
towards a shallow bowl. Each time we reached the bowl, she changed the song.
Once the answer came from ahead; once from behind Ka-Dhavar; on what I
counted as the seventh passage, from an opening I had not seen. I could see three
ways out, though I had counted two before. I left the file to examine a crack beside
one opening. I was sure I had seen it before.
    “No,” Ka-Elun said, without turning.
    “I did not speak.”
    “You leaned toward certainty. There is no crack you have seen before. There
are only cracks that repeat. You may observe, Va-Kailan. You may not follow
yourself.”
    I returned to the file.
    In the next chamber three doorways faced us. Ka-Elun sang three rising notes
towards them. The returns reached us one after another.


194
                                                                                Trial


    Ka-Elun passed behind Ka-Raedin and covered his eyes with both hands. “Eyes
closed. All of you.” She lowered the hood.
    When the light returned I waited, then looked. Her hands still covered Ka-
Raedin’s eyes, his head tilted back towards her. The others still had their eyes
closed. The chamber stretched away to my right; five doorways stood where I
remembered three. My heel still rested in the same hollow. Ka-Elun sang the three
notes again. Five returns crossed the chamber.
    Ka-Cheryn opened her eyes. “Ka-Elun. Your hands.” She watched until Faith
released him. Ka-Raedin straightened his robe, and Ka-Elun took up the lamp.
    At the next turn I heard water running on the left. Farther along, its note
thinned beside a hollow in the wall. I turned towards it and put my hand on the
stone. Cold moved through one narrow joint into my palm.
    Ka-Elun let me lead for a few steps. When I stopped she came up beside me
in the dark. “A Light is given the eye,” she said, barely above a breath. “You were
handed it as a boy, and you are good at it, and it is not the best of what is in you.
Faith hears. You heard.”
    “Before the Beast, silence,” she said, at the wall where I had stopped — and
the answer came from behind me, from the Keepers themselves: “Before silence,
prayer. Before prayer, breath. Before breath, fear.” The seam opened where my
ear had set me, and there was distance now where there had been none. “Now,”
she said, “Light may look.”
    We came into a circular room. Ten doors ringed it, five beneath carved arches
and five flush with the wall. Nine were shut; the Labyrinth stood open behind us.
Each office mark appeared twice.
    At the centre stood a king cut from dark stone, his crown and hands left whole,
his face chiselled away. A city in gold circled his feet. The closed arch of Faith stood
at his back; his erased face was set towards the bridge and, beyond it, the Gate.
    Ka-Elun sang once more, a measure she had not used on the descent. Her voice
returned from the wall before him. Stone moved and opened the way into the
antechamber.
    The bridge was a tongue of stone wide enough for thirty men abreast, running
level across a gulf of open air to the far wall. Above, the dark climbed higher than
the Mountain stands above the sand, and higher again. Below, to either side, the
floor fell away into two long pits. Out of them rose the pillars, scarred and past
counting, their heads lost past where the light could climb. Around their bases,
picked out in gold, cities crossed bands of carved sand. I could not tell whether
they were emerging or being drowned.


                                                                                   195
Homo Noxius




   (Paul): — No. Stop. This does not exist.
   (Kylian): — After all this time, this is where you stop believing my account?
   (Paul): — This is bullshit. Sarah. Come on!
   (Sarah): — No, Paul. I was there.


    The two long walls were the Mountain itself, broken rock on either side of
the bridge. The far wall was built, and the bridge ran straight at the Gate of the
Hall of Cataclysm. It rose the height of a great tree and spanned the width of the
bridge, every part of it mechanism. Chains thick as tree-trunks entered it out of
the rock and climbed into slots above. Five rows of five signs had been cut across
its face, figures I failed to read; low to one side an empty slot waited in the iron.
At its centre was the Beast’s face from the Pendulum, a great cat with round ears
flattened and five teeth bared beneath almost human eyes.
    Across the gulf to my left, on the wall at right angles to the Gate, spread the
Cataclysm gravure, five-and-twenty paces to a side. The Mountain stood above
the Beast, which strained upward out of Hell with its fists at the roots of the Earth.
Five figures stood apart in five arches, each holding one of its chains. Beyond the
Mountain the Outsiders crowded under the open eye of Heaven; flame passed
through their cities and great waves broke over them. At the foot of the ruin the
people went down into the stone.
    In the antechamber, five seats faced the accused mark, cut from the chamber’s
own stone, spaced so no Keeper could easily whisper to another. At the centre lay
a flat stone table where objects could be placed so all could see them and no single
hand receive them. Nothing lay upon it.
    Ka-Dhavar brought me to the mark with one touch at the shoulder. He used
no restraint and stayed within reach until the five had taken their seats. Ka-Leth
of Justice stood beside the central stone, his own hands empty, for Justice keeps
clear of the record and only speaks into it. At the wall stood Va-Sheva of Shield
and Sa-Tavan of Justice with the tablet on which the record would be kept.
    “This antechamber opens under seal,” Ka-Leth said, and the words stayed
small in the vastness. “No Kahir divulges what belongs solely to its own sealed
charge. No Keeper seeks total knowledge. The hearing binds the five without mak-
ing one hand whole. Before judgement, the chamber remembers why judgement
is divided.”


196
                                                                             Trial


   Faith read both Books. Ka-Elun unwrapped the first scroll and smoothed its
cloth across her knees. She laid the roll open upon it, easing one curled edge flat
with the side of her hand. She rested her hands beside it.
   “The Book of the Beast,” Ka-Leth said, and Ka-Elun began.
   She spoke from memory, looking across the seats. Her voice stayed low. She
carried the long sentences over several breaths, keeping their pace, and at the ends
of passages waited for the faint return from beyond us. I followed with my lips,
taking breath where she did.

      And the curse went forth upon the Outsiders, and they
      wandered under the eye of Heaven; and the Earth hid them
      not, neither did the waters keep them, nor the Mountain give
      them shade. And they multiplied in the open land, and their
      hunger multiplied with them; and brother rose against
      brother, and city rose against city, and king rose against king.
      And they made war without measure, and famine without
      pity, and prisons without mercy; and they bound the weak,
      and burned the innocent, and betrayed the hand that fed
      them.
      And God looked upon the Outsiders, and saw the works of
      their hands; and behold, their cruelty was greater than the
      curse that had been laid upon them. For they tortured for
      pleasure, and conquered for pride, and lied with holy words,
      and slew their children for the hunger of their fathers. And
      God was wroth, and God said, The evil of the Outsiders is
      deep, and their hunger hath no end; and if they find the
      Mountain, they shall not spare the Oasis, nor the Temple, nor
      the children beneath My shadow.

      And God gave unto the Oasis a great defence, and set power in
      the Keepers thereof. Unto the Keeper of Light He gave the
      Dance of Light, that the hidden ways might be revealed to the
      appointed eye. Unto the Keeper of Balance He gave the
      Movement of the Waters, to raise and withhold the deep. Unto
      the Keeper of Justice He gave the Key, the Number, and the
      Binding of the Chains, that no burden move without order
      and witness. Unto the Keeper of Shield He gave the Sword and
      the Seal, to close the Mountain against the wrath it had
      awakened. And unto the Keeper of Faith He gave the Prayer
      and the Listening of Stone, that the holy word might be spoken
      in rhythm, and the trembling of the Earth understood.
      And God said unto the Keepers, Keep these secrets apart, and
      let no one hand hold the whole; for the power that defendeth

                                                                               197
Homo Noxius


      the Oasis may also devour the Earth. And He taught each
      Keeper his portion, and sealed the rest from him. And each
      was strong in his charge, and weak without the others.

      And God said, If the Outsiders come again, and if they find
      the Mountain, and if they lay their hands upon the Temple,
      then shall the Keepers stand as one and unleash the Beast
      upon the Earth. And the poisoned waters shall rise, and the
      volcanoes shall erupt, and the ground shall break beneath the
      feet of the enemy, and the sea shall climb over their cities; and
      the Outsiders shall taste the cruelty they gave upon the world.
      But if the Keepers fail, or if one hand seize the whole, then the
      Beast shall know neither faithful nor stranger, and Hell shall
      not distinguish between the guilty and the guarded.

   Ka-Elun closed the first scroll. Ka-Leth opened the second. “The Book of the
Cataclysm,” he said. Ka-Elun read on. The Book of the Beast says what God will
do; this one says what was done. It tells again what the first had promised, now as
a thing done: the enemy coming with banners and false prayers, the five bringing
forth each the secret given him and standing apart as one, and the answer of Hell.

      And the five stood apart as one, and no one hand held the
      whole. And God remembered His word, and Hell answered.

   At the end Ka-Elun read:

      And they wept for the world that had been saved and lost in
      the same hour. And God said unto them, Dwell no more in the
      open, but enter the Mountain, and keep the Temple, and
      remember the price of being seen. And the Temple was sealed,
      and the people went beneath the stone.

   Ka-Elun closed the second scroll, and no one spoke.
   Ka-Leth set out the charges: Breach of Seal, Bearing Forbidden Proof, Unau-
thorised Revelation in potential and act. “The law judges not only the fire that
has burned, but the place where fire was carried.” And then the frame that closed
the room like a lid: “No cross-Kahir knowledge may be divulged. No sealed name
spoken. Any attempt to defend by renewing the breach will be stopped.”
   “How do I answer what cannot be spoken?”
   “With what may be spoken.”
   “And if what may be spoken is not enough?”
   “Then you begin to understand the charge.”

                                                                               Trial


    He read eight facts into the record, and I contested no sequence. The route
had been reconstructed from Shield’s seals and my own account: unauthorised
movement through the exterior ways; defeat of the first archive lock; entry into
a sealed Shield chamber; handling of restricted material; removal of two records
and one woven object; carriage through inhabited galleries; concealment of one
record beneath a Light seal; surrender of the woven object and the other record
to Shield. Sa-Tavan set each fact into the tablet.
    Ka-Leth turned to Va-Sheva. She spoke to the centre stone and kept her hands
behind her back. “What danger did you judge?”
    “Proof travelling by a credible bearer.”
    “Not treason?”
    “No standing to judge treason.”
    “Did he surrender the proof?”
    “He returned the woven object and presented the torn record to Shield. The
second record remained beneath his Light seal.”
    “Did he disclose its contents to another person?”
    “No record contents were disclosed beyond Shield. The Keeper of Light re-
ceived questions of law and no fact.”
    “Do those facts remove the danger?”
    Va-Sheva’s thumb pressed into the opposite palm. “No. All three objects had
crossed every seal meant to contain them. A credible bearer carried them among
the people for two days and placed one inside another office. Surrender stopped
his route. It did not unmake it.”
    She stepped back. Ka-Leth asked whether I contested her evidence. I said no.
    “There are only two outcomes lawful in this chamber,” Ka-Leth said. “Death,
or Exception.”
    “Before the offices, the accused may speak within the boundaries he has been
given,” Ka-Leth said.
    “An unexplained beam is Light’s to follow. A beam from beyond the Moun-
tain may be the first sign of an Outsider Shield has failed to detect; until its source
is known, Light may be the only office capable of finding the threat and warning
the Mountain.” I waited before continuing. “I have come to suspect that the
Mountain is the victim of a great crime, possibly treason. Had I returned all three
objects, one office would have held the suspected act, the evidence of it and the
power to decide whether the Mountain would ever hear either.”


       Let no one hand hold the whole.

   “Upon discovering a suspected breach or worse, I went to Ka-Raedin of Light

intentions: the law does not trade a total loss for a comfort. Death.”

       Let no sign be carried from its chamber, nor proof borne

       people, but a secret made to wander shall become fire among

    “The people saw him at prayer in the Hall of Ceremony for months. Faith can

    “Light.” Ka-Raedin came forward, and I stopped breathing. “He is useful. He

lawful until Justice removed the office. The disc had rested against my ribs since

shape of a great cloud, taller than the Mountain, that turned the sand to glass and
poisoned the wind for a generation. Four weeks’ walk, exactly north of here, at a
place the Outsiders call Reggane. One of the few great seals in living memory was
set on that day, presented as a drill — because the fire burnt so bright that Xhian,
formerly Ka-Xhian, Keeper of Shield, lost his sight observing it, and because with
it came an earth-shaking sound too great for Faith to miss and go on calling itself
Faith. Fourteen years on, the same office sealed us once more against a danger out
of that same quarter; that too the children were told was a drill or a game.”

    Ka-Leth looked in turn towards Light, Balance, Faith and Shield. The five seats

    Ka-Leth had spent forty years inside the record. Most wrongs came to him
with a known shape and cost: a miscounted measure, a stolen ration, a struck
child. Another kind of matter had entered the record perhaps a handful of times
in eleven thousand years — without precedent, too large and too little understood
for the law to measure its reach. Almost every one had ended in catastrophe.
    “You will not have heard this very old story. A single word was changed

thrashing of a condemned man who has felt the water climb to his mouth. The

    I had four days, no office and no accusation. The word useful had survived

    And then to Shield, and the whole night narrowed to the width of the next

    “This is nowhere in the law,” Ka-Leth said.

asks instead that he be used.”
    Ka-Leth was quiet a long moment. “Understand what is before us, and what is

outward where it has only ever sealed within. If it is granted, it is granted by the
five offices explicitly, each in its own voice. Speak the word, or the sentence already
spoken stands.”

this much: light that passes a narrow opening comes out changed, and changes

Homo Noxius


what it falls on. His path, thrown so far, will mark the Mountain. Exception. My
word does not restore his name.”
     Ka-Elun kept one hand on the torn mantle. “Give him a year. His foot will
strike the Mountain’s note ten thousand times. He will miss it, then find it again;
no tread keeps one beat. A small thing returning upon the stone’s own note is
what wakes the deep. I do not trust his beat. Exception.”
     Ka-Cheryn turned a hand over as when a column would not come even. “You
would put him outside to keep the inside clean. The sum never falls. Disorder
swept from one room is paid for with more in the next; it gathers, warms and
leans back on the door. There is no away. Exception, against my count.”
     Ka-Dhavar remained standing. “I have set every seal the Mountain owns. None
is tight. When knowledge has passed into another mouth, I cannot bring it back by
shutting a gate. Some has gone ahead of him already. Shield can slow the crossing.
I will keep the seals and the watch. Exception.”
     Four votes had been spoken. The sentence stood until Justice gave the fifth.
Ka-Leth kept his hands above the centre stone and looked past all of us, to the
black air.
     “I never let myself know the world was a sphere. I know this much of closed
surfaces: some carry a throat, a loop that cannot be tightened to a point. If every
circuit can be tightened to a point without leaving the surface or tearing it, that
surface is a sphere, however it is bent. We are sending this man towards what we
call outside. A seamless world keeps no outside. If his road closes, it closes on us.
If it stays open, whose law governs the road?” He laid his empty hands flat on the
centre stone. “Exception — and on my head be the throat I could not find.”


   (Paul): — I should ask my brother John about that.


   The five had spoken it. Ka-Leth returned, last, to say aloud what they had
made.
   “It is one voice,” he said. “The sentence of death is stayed, and its cover kept
whole. To the Mountain you are the devout boy the Labyrinth took; on the fourth
day they will grieve you, and you will let them, and never once, while you live, send
them word that the grief is misspent. What you are from this breath has no name
in any Book. This record will call it Keeper of Shadow; the name grants no office
and no seat. You go out under Shield, by the door we do not name above the deep,
and you send back nothing but the truth, through Va-Sheva, until the five release

210
                                                                             Trial


you or the desert does. You asked to be made useful at a distance. The Mountain
grants you the distance. Do not mistake it for a pardon. It is the longest leash the
law has ever paid out, and it is a leash still.”
   No one asked him to explain the sphere. I carried the words without their
sense. Ka-Leth gave Va-Sheva a sealed copy of the charges and the terms he had
just spoken. He kept the hearing tablet.


    Ka-Raedin came within an arm’s length and lowered his voice. “Your seal held.”
He let that stand between us, then said, “You are alive.”
    “Yes, Keeper.”
    “You will be tempted to think that being useful is the same as being forgiven.
It is the opposite. You have bought yourself time, and nothing else — time to
become capable of carrying what you forced yourself to see. Go and be the eye.
Shadow is Shield’s answer to you now. The eye is also, and will remain… Light’s.”
He went to wait beside the bridge.
    Ka-Elun stopped me before I could follow him. “Do not make a gospel of what
has happened to you,” she said.
    “No, Keeper.”
    “That will be harder than silence. You have a martyr’s material now, and a
preacher’s tongue, and neither the office nor the innocence to be trusted with
either. Carry the truth. Do not preach it. The Beast knows neither faithful nor
stranger. Neither, now, does your story. Give the account to the ear the five have
named, and to no one else.” She lifted the lamp.
    I stepped towards the bridge. No one put a hand on me.

                                 Horizon




    We went back up the Labyrinth together, the five Keepers, Va-Sheva and I, with
Sa-Tavan following Justice. We had collected him in the circular room beyond the
bridge, his tablet still wrapped against his chest. Ka-Elun sang at the divisions. On
the steeper turns I put a hand against the wall and let the person ahead draw away
before I followed.
    At the carved prayer Ka-Elun raised her lamp. The stone opened onto the Hall
of Prayers, and Ka-Raedin retrieved the lamp he had left there. Sa-Tavan went
with Justice; the other Keepers took their own ways. I watched Ka-Raedin until
the turn hid him. Va-Sheva waited beside me.
    I had never seen the Mountain this empty. The doors along the gallery were
shut, including the little service door people usually propped with a bread basket.
Our feet sounded all the way to the next turn.
    “May I pray before the Pendulum? One last time, please.”
    She looked down the gallery, then led me to the Hall of Ceremony.
    The Pendulum passed beneath us. I knelt at the rail where I had knelt among
hundreds of others. The carved leaves beneath my palms had worn smooth,
though their curled edges still caught my fingertips. I eased my robe clear of my
heels and laid my forehead against my hands. The stone held a little warmth
through the cloth at my knees.
    I said the prayer my father had taught me. At its end I thanked God that I was
alive.

                                        221
Homo Noxius


    I raised my head and stayed on my knees for another passage of the Pendulum.
The Beast’s face caught the light. Va-Sheva waited until I got up.
    She took a lamp from its bracket by a service door on Shield’s side of the
Hall and led me down into the passage towards the second ridge. At a division
I recognised the slope leading towards the archive. The opening with Faith and
Shield joined above it was there too. I stopped short of it.
    Va-Sheva held the lamp farther along the passage. The door ahead bore three
Shield seals, cut one over another, the oldest sunk deep into the stone. I had
turned away from it on my first visit. She drew a long iron key from inside her
belt, Shield’s mark cut into its bow. She fitted it beneath the seals and turned it;
bolts scraped back inside the stone. She recovered the key and pushed the door
open far enough for us to pass.
    Beyond it the passage widened into a room with four pillars on either side.
Two stone benches faced each other across a low lamp-holder. High above them,
a round hole in the roof held a few stars. The corridor continued beyond the
opposite bench.
    “Who sits here?” I asked.
    “Ka-Xhian received the woman in your photograph here. His Veil stood be-
hind those pillars.”
    I looked at the bench she indicated. Va-Sheva set the lamp in its holder and
opened a wall cupboard. She brought out two packs, water skins and sand wraps.
Before she let me put them on, she searched the folds of my robe, the stitching at
my cuffs and the soles of my sandals. Ka-Raedin already held my Light token; her
fingers checked its empty place.
    “Nothing for your household?”
    My mother’s face came before me with the ordinary exactness of a meal-place:
her thumb smoothing a cracked bowl, the small dark mark beside one nail. “Noth-
ing.”
    Va-Sheva covered the white knot at my wrist with dark cloth, bound it with a
cord and tied my belt again. I watched her fit Justice’s packet inside her own pack.
    “They are still counting?” I asked.
    “The doors stay shut until you are out. Then they will be told you were seen
entering the Labyrinth. Faith will send pilgrims to look for you.”
    “And on the fourth day.”
    “Yes.”
    She lifted the pack onto my shoulders. I pulled the straps even and followed
her into the opposite corridor. Three sealed crossings stood along it, the mark of


222
                                                                            Horizon


Shield joining the stone leaves at each. Beyond the last, the passage ran straight
into darkness.
   At its end she covered the lamp and knocked against the rock. The opening
came slowly, bringing cold air across my face. A young man waited outside, his
head and mouth wrapped against the cold.
   “Amastan,” Va-Sheva said.
   He gave me a brief look, took the lamp from her and put it inside the opening.
Then he helped her draw the stone shut.


    Three camels knelt in a hollow below the entrance. Amastan tightened a strap,
moved a water skin from one load to another and beckoned me to the smallest
animal. I climbed on as he showed me. When it rose I pitched forward over its
neck; Va-Sheva caught my pack and pushed me back into the saddle.
    For a while I could see the Mountain whenever I turned. Then we went down
between two ridges and I lost it. I twisted farther, trying to see over the rise, until
the camel stumbled and I had to catch the saddle. Amastan clicked his tongue and
led us on.
    We travelled at night. By the first dawn I could scarcely close my knees. Un-
der an overhang Amastan spread a desert-coloured cloth across the opening and
pinned it with stones; I lay down before he had finished. At dusk Va-Sheva woke
me with a water skin against my cheek. The next night an engine passed beyond
a ridge. Amastan led the animals into a hollow and we waited there until he was
willing to move.
    Before the third dawn we reached a dry well, its lip cracked and its rope gone.
A dead thorn tree leaned over fallen stones. Amastan unloaded the camels and
took them round the ridge to feed. Va-Sheva sat against the well and loosened
the wrapping at her neck. I lowered myself beside her, slowly enough to spare my
knees.
    “I made you stand against your own Keeper.”
    “Kay, I sat where the sequence required me and scarcely spoke. He has heard
me disagree before. We both put the Mountain’s safety first. I will survive this.”
    For a while we watched the light grow along the ridge. She had folded her wraps
beneath her head and closed her eyes. I moved a stone that was pressing into my
leg.
    “Will I see you again?”
    “Your reports come through me.”
    “That is not what I asked.”

                                                                                  223
Homo Noxius


    She opened her eyes. “I have to get you to them first.”
    “You chose to bring me yourself.”
    “Kay. You were entrusted to me.”
    “I wanted you to choose me.”
    She pulled the wraps from behind her head and began folding them. I waited
for her to answer. Before she had finished, an engine sounded beyond the ridge.
She got up with the cloth still in her hands.
    The Toyota came over the rise slowly, large tyres working through the loose
stones. Two people sat behind its glass. The man beside the driver saw Va-Sheva
and raised a hand. She raised hers before the vehicle had stopped.
    “Jean-Charles,” she said.
    He climbed out, a sun-lined man in close-fitting clothes, with a short blade
at his belt and a row of closed pockets down his coat. Va-Sheva dropped her
wraps and ran past me. She jumped into his arms, locked her legs round his waist
and kissed him, her hands behind his head. He caught her under the thighs and
stumbled back against the vehicle. She was laughing when she drew away to look
at him; he tried to speak, and she kissed him again.
    I went back to the well for her wraps. I would not ask her to choose me again.
    When he set her down, she kept a palm flat against his chest. He covered it with
his own and held it there before reaching into a pocket for a bright, flat box tied
with pink ribbon.
    “Pistachio and rose. They travelled badly. Eat them tonight or they die.”
    Va-Sheva took it with both hands. The driver’s door opened, and she put the
box inside her coat. I held out her wraps. She took them without looking at me.
    The woman who stepped out had short pale hair and a grey jacket without an
office sign. A narrow cord disappeared into her collar. Va-Sheva called her Va-Elise.
They spoke together beside the vehicle while Jean-Charles loaded our packs.
    I went towards the camels. Jean-Charles caught my shoulder with his right
hand and turned me back towards the vehicle.
    “With us. Leave the animals to him.”
    He spoke my language with unfamiliar stresses. His hand was gone before I
had decided whether to resist it. Va-Sheva came to me, loosened the dark cord at
my wrist and drew it away without disturbing the cloth over the white knot. She
laid it on Justice’s sealed packet and handed both to Va-Elise, who put them in
her bag.
    “Get in,” Va-Elise said. “I will show you the belt.”



224
                                                                            Horizon


    From the back seat I could see Va-Sheva in the little mirror on the door. She
had taken Jean-Charles a few paces away. I heard the engine and Va-Elise moving
things beside me; their words did not reach the vehicle.
    Va-Sheva caught his hand as he began to speak and brought it low against her
belly. She held it there, looking into his face, then nodded. He stared at her. His
mouth opened into a smile so sudden that she laughed and caught his other hand.
He bent to kiss her; she met him, then drew back and looked towards the ridge
beyond which lay the Mountain.
    She turned her hand in his, showing the black line of Seal dye. His smile faltered.
For a moment he kept both hands closed around hers. She said something else,
and when he answered she put a finger against his lips. He kissed it. She was crying
now, still smiling, wiping her cheek with the hand he had released.
    “What has he said to her?” I asked.
    Va-Elise leaned across me and drew the belt out. “Lift your arm.”
    I did, and lost the mirror behind her shoulder. By the time she had fastened
it, Va-Sheva was walking towards Amastan. Va-Elise returned to the wheel. Jean-
Charles watched Va-Sheva go, then took the front passenger seat. I moved closer
to the opposite side of the seat.
    “You are safer than you feel,” he said.
    The engine deepened. In the mirror I watched Amastan hold a camel steady
for her. She turned once towards the vehicle, but we were moving by then and I
could not see her face.

   (Kylian): — No. I ... I am an idiot. Let’s just carry on.


   An hour after the well, Jean-Charles raised a black instrument to his eye and
adjusted it with his thumb.
   “One vehicle. About three kilometres. Coming towards us.”
   “Shit. Who the fuck is that?” Va-Elise asked.
   He watched a little longer. “Stop the car.”
   The Toyota stopped so hard that the belt caught me across the chest and drove
into my shoulder. I gripped it, trying to pull enough slack to breathe.
   “If there’s one in front, there may be another behind us. Turn round.”




226
                                                                         Horizon


    Va-Elise swung the Toyota across the flat. Jean-Charles reached down beside
his seat and brought a long case across his knees. As he opened it, she pointed
through the front glass.
    “There. Dust.”
    A pale column rose ahead of us, on the ground we had just crossed. Behind
us, the first vehicle was now gaining on us. A man stood in its open back with his
hands on a mounted gun.
    Jean-Charles took a rifle with a glass tube above it from the case. He passed me
two padded cups joined by a band and touched his ears. I pulled them over mine.
Something struck the rear door with a hard metallic crack.
    He turned on the passenger seat, one knee pressed into its cushion, and
brought the rifle past my face. “Careful. This will be loud.”
    The shot broke the glass behind us. I flinched as wind struck the back of my
head. Jean-Charles caught my upper arm and settled the rifle across my shoulder.
    “Stay still. Breathe in, then hold it when I say now.”
    I drew air in. His cheek was close to mine.
    “Now.”
    The rifle jolted against my shoulder. I held my breath until he squeezed my arm
and told me I could let it out. Twice more he said “Now”, and twice more I held
still while he fired. Va-Elise kept her eyes ahead.
    He lifted the rifle from my shoulder and watched through its glass. “All three
dead. Turn round.”
    The belt pulled across the sore place as Va-Elise turned us back. Through the
broken window I saw the second vehicle veer away across the flat. Its dust grew
fainter.
    We passed the first vehicle on our way back along our original track. It stood
crooked on its wheels. Two heads hung forward in the cab; a third man lay across
the open back beneath the gun. A red band circled his head wrap. His jacket and
trousers were different colours of camouflage.
    “Who were they?” I asked.
    Jean-Charles lowered the rifle across his knees. “Kidnappers.”
    “For whom?”
    “Whoever pays. Daesh buys captives across the border.”
    Glass had gathered in my lap. I tried to brush it away and he caught my wrist.
    “Leave it. Are you hurt?”
    I shook my head. He took off the padded cups. My ears rang, and blood was
running from one knuckle.


                                                                               227
Homo Noxius


   We drove until the sun was high. At a halt Jean-Charles shook out my robe
and bound my hand while Va-Elise watched the empty ground behind us. He
tightened the dressing and waited for me to get back in. Va-Elise drove on.
   By late afternoon we reached a road lined with houses. People stood in their
doorways. A man pushed a wheeled bin past our window and never looked up. I
turned to watch him until the vehicle carried us round a corner.
   Va-Elise drove through a metal gate into a yard. Jean-Charles closed it behind
us and began clearing the broken glass while she let me into the house, locked the
door and put her bag on a table. The lights were small glass bulbs fixed to the wall.
I held my palm near one while she went through the cupboards for clothes.
   “You can look later,” she said. “Wash first.”
   They gave me water in a room where I could close the door. I washed the dust
from my face and arms. It gathered in the bottom of the basin; when Jean-Charles
showed me the release, it went away through a hole too small for my smallest
finger.
   The shirt closed with discs pushed through slits in the cloth. I missed one
and had to undo the others to put the edges level. The trousers showed each leg
separately. I turned twice before the metal wall, searching the folded things for an
outer wrapping.
   When I returned, Jean-Charles pulled the back of my collar free where I had
folded it under. Va-Elise handed me a pair of spectacles.
   “My sight is good.”
   “The lenses are clear. We want people to remember an ordinary young man
with glasses.”
   She sat me in front of a camera, then took the spectacles off again. On the table
were a telephone, a world map spread wider than my arms and a passport waiting
for my image.
   “Outside, your name is Kylian. Say it.”
   I said it. She corrected the sound, took the picture and wrote the name in the
outside hand. I copied the letters until she covered her version and I could make
them without it.
   I put down the pen. Va-Elise was putting the photograph against the passport.
She gave me back the spectacles when she had finished, then named the places on
the map: Switzerland, Geneva, Saanen. I followed her finger across the printed
water.
   “Someone may take this from you and look at your face,” she said, closing the
passport. “Let him. Stay beside me. If he speaks, wait for me to answer.”


228
                                                                           Horizon


    Jean-Charles lifted the lid from a dish of chicken, its skin browned and shining
with spice. Steam rose from yellow rice heaped with soft onions. Va-Elise spooned
the cooking juices over my plate; beside her waited a tray of small pastries glossy
with honey, their ends packed with crushed almonds.
    Jean-Charles took his glass and raised it a little.
    “Measured. Witnessed,” he said, grinning.
    Va-Elise looked at him. “Do not.”
    “Sorry. Bad joke,” he added, and set it down. “We don’t make a ceremony of
supper, so eat while it’s hot.”
    I laughed, and had to put down my glass. Va-Elise shook her head and went on
serving.
    “You did well in the car,” he said. “Kept your head. You’re safe now.”
    The skin cracked between my teeth. Beneath it the meat came away wet with
lemon and the juices that had soaked into the rice. I ate until Va-Elise reached for
my plate, then held it out for more. Jean-Charles was still on his first helping.
    He pushed the pastries towards me. Honey ran over my thumb when I bit
into one; the thin layers broke against my lips, releasing the almonds. I licked my
thumb and picked up the flakes from my plate.
    After the meal Va-Elise cleared a place beside my glass and set a book there. “We
were told you might enjoy this. It’s about how people out here have studied light.”
    I cleaned the honey from my fingers. The cover showed a beam bending
through a block of glass. I followed its passage with my finger. Below the title,
a strip of paper bore the words in careful Mountain hand.
    Light — David Burnie.
    I read the title twice. I could feel myself grinning, and then my eyes filled. Va-
Elise waited while I wiped them on my sleeve.
    “Thank you,” I said.
    I opened it at the beginning. Thin sheets of handwriting had been bound
between its printed pages. There were photographs of objects with their names
and descriptions. I recognised the candle and the mirror, and lingered over instru-
ments with polished tubes and small wheels. Each label had its translation.
    “You translated all of it?”
    “The whole book. We kept the English beside it. Your tutor will help you with
that.”
    I began with Light, myth, and magic. The opening paragraph asked what
would happen if the sun failed to rise: water would freeze, plants and animals



                                                                                 229
Homo Noxius


would die. I read on to its assurance that this could not happen, then turned the
page.
   On the next spread, Prometheus carried fire down from the mountain where
the gods lived. The caption said he had stolen it for men. I read the paragraph
again.


   (Kylian): — Do you remember when we spoke of aletheia? “Then a god gives men
   fire whole, undivided and in every hand, and is chained to a rock, where a bird opens
   his liver each day.”
   (Paul): — Prometheus.
   (Kylian): — This was where I first read his name. The tutor told me the rest later.


    I took a loose thread from the edge of my cuff and laid it beneath his name.
“May I take it with me?”
    “It’s yours, Kylian.”
    Va-Elise took the telephone into the next room and shut the door. Jean-Charles
sat opposite me and asked about the road out of the Mountain. He wanted the
marks on the door I had left unopened, the soundless room’s position beside it,
whether the Mountain had stayed under seal until I was out. I answered with the
book on my knees. He waited when I stopped, then asked about the next crossing.
    Near the end he said, “No need to trouble Elise with any of that. She writes
everything down, and a man should keep some of his own weather.”
    I nodded and kept my hand on the book. When Va-Elise returned, I carried it
to bed myself and lay down with my shoes still on. She woke me before it was light
again. The Toyota had a new rear window. Va-Elise took the wheel; Jean-Charles
sat beside her.


   At the airfield Va-Elise handed papers through a small window. A man came
out and opened a gate; our vehicle stopped beside a white aircraft with a gold cross
dividing green and black on its tail.
   “Jamaican diplomatic flight,” Va-Elise said. “To Geneva. Stay with me.”
   The captain came down the steps to meet us, a lean, dark-skinned man with
his hair tied high behind his head and gold bars on the shoulders of his white shirt.
He shook Jean-Charles’s hand and kept it while they laughed. Then he offered me
his.
   “Capt’n Billy.”

230
                                                                           Horizon


   I gave him the name Va-Elise had told me to use. He repeated it while Jean-
Charles lifted our bags from the vehicle and handed them to one of the crew.
   “Back to the desert for me,” Jean-Charles said. “Stay with Elise.”
   He got behind the wheel. Billy led Va-Elise and me up the steps; from the top
I watched the vehicle turn towards the gate. Wide leather seats faced one another
across a table. Va-Elise put her papers on it. Billy rested a hand on the back of
my seat while she translated between us, his English into Mountain speech, my
answers back to him.
   “Next time you come to Jamaica. You wanna go snorkelling? You wanna eat
lobster? You wanna smoke weed? You can do all dees tings. Just let Capt’n Billy
know.”
   Va-Elise repeated the English slowly before giving me each part. I asked what
snorkelling was. She put two fingers beside her eyes, pointed downward and said
that I could breathe while I looked under the sea. Billy watched the demonstration
and nodded.
   Va-Elise looked up from her papers. “Geneva first, Billy.”
   “Yes, ma’am.” He pulled once on my fastened belt, then went forward to the
other pilot and shut the door.
   The floor tilted beneath my feet; pressure closed my ears. The earth withdrew
below the window.
   “Do not look down until I say,” Va-Elise told me.
   I watched the fastener of my belt. A loose end of the strap lifted each time I
breathed. When I looked up, the line of land beyond her shoulder lay far below
us. Water reached from it to the edge of the window. She put her papers down
and talked while I kept my eyes on her.
   Somewhere over that water Va-Elise put a cup beside my hand and returned to
her papers. I waited for the drink to spill. Its surface trembled, settled, and stayed
almost level while the land passed slowly below us. I drank without taking my eyes
from the rim. After a while I put the cup down and let go of the seat.
   I opened the book at the thread and asked Va-Elise to read the English beneath
Prometheus. She read it slowly, then again as I followed the printed words with
my finger.
   The next chapter was Shadows. A caption about an eclipse named Jamaica.
Opposite, beside a shadow theatre, I found a portrait and asked her the man’s
name.
   “Johannes Kepler.”



                                                                                 231
Homo Noxius


   She read me the short account of his work on light and shadows. I asked her to
say his name again.
   “Is Jamaica across this sea?” I asked.
   “Across the Atlantic. Much farther.”
   She took out her telephone and showed me a photograph of a small boat in
clear blue water. I could see its shadow on the sand beneath it.
   “Could we go there?”
   “Geneva first, Kylian.”
   She left the picture open beside my book.
   At Geneva a vehicle met us beside the aircraft and took us to a helicopter. “The
family’s,” Va-Elise said. She held my sleeve against its beating wind until I reached
the door.
   From it I saw mountains with roads cut across their faces, houses standing on
the slopes and water gathered between them. Church towers rose above the roofs.
A boat crossed the lake below us, leaving two lines that spread until I could no
longer distinguish them. We landed on a helipad beside this house above the water.
The air smelled of pine, metal and bread.

on people who never heard our names. Who is left above to read how carefully we
decided?
(Paul): — I cannot answer that. The reel keeps what the five said. It does not keep what
you could see from the wall.
(Kailan): — You were in the room. Your machine ran the whole while. Ask the Keepers.
Ask Sarah, ask Va-Sheva — they spoke. I stood with my hands bound and said almost
nothing.
(Paul): — Va-Sheva refuses me; Sarah turns away; the Keepers are turning air and
counting fish. I have asked them. At present I have my account, and I was occupied
with the machine for much of it.
(Kailan): — I have a shift on the vents. Before it, I need to lie still. There is nothing in
me to give you.
                                          [—]


                                         241
Homo Noxius

   (Paul): — Then leave the hearing. The road from Blackwood. Could you give me that?
   (Kailan): — … You kept the record. Give them the road yourself. I will take it up inside
   the Mountain. Paul. Let me rest.


   I am Paul Morgan. This is my account of the return. My training is in strata,
middens, worked flint and bone laid down in silt forty thousand years ago. The
dates and intervals here come from the reel boxes and my notebooks. Where I
stopped recording, I have used my memory; where I could not understand a
speaker, I name the person who supplied the words.
   At dusk I came down the steps of Blackwood with one bag holding my note-
books, interview reels and travelling recorder. The dressing Kailan had put round
my right hand caught under the strap. I freed it as Va-Elise waved me toward the
helicopter. Behind her, men carried cases through doors I had never seen open. A
sheet of paper crossed the lawn, rose into the rotor wind and vanished over the
roof.
   Kailan waited at the door while a crewman loaded Sarah’s chair. She was still
giving orders when we lifted. Her right thumb shook against the headset until she
closed it beneath her left hand.
   She called the outer stations separately. Two calls ended with her spoken ac-
knowledgment. During the third she repeated a number, waited and marked a
cross in her book. She began the fourth with the pencil still against the page.
Kailan sat opposite me; at each bank I drew the bag back against my ankles.
   At Geneva, Urs stopped at the hangar’s red line and we boarded the private
aircraft. There were beds and a shower. Food arrived through a door I failed to
notice, and I ate all of it. Kailan watched the window. Sarah ate half a piece of toast
and slept upright. Once she opened her eyes. “Finish your meal, Mr Morgan. I do
not know when any of us will eat properly again.”
   Va-Elise spread her maps at the table. Where the road ended, she pointed out
our meeting place with Amastan. “Beyond this, his ground.”
   “If he cannot reach it, keep them under cover,” Sarah said. “Nobody goes
looking. Keep enough fuel to take the drivers back.”
   I lay down. We flew through the night; I woke as we began our descent.


  At dawn we came down onto old colonial tarmac. Four massive black tactical
4x4s stood beyond the lamps, engines turning, windows dark above the high slabs



242
                                                                          Return


of their doors. Jean-Charles came from the second to meet us. Kailan spoke his
name and raised a hand. Va-Elise was already giving the drivers their maps.
   A tall blonde woman stood beside the driver’s door, her hair cropped above
her ears, a fitted black shirt tucked into her field trousers. She handed Va-Elise a
roll of satellite prints.
   Men loaded water and medical cases while Sarah refused the chair. Kailan
stayed one step below her until she reached the tarmac. She stood there to recover
her breath, watching the baggage go in. Jean-Charles checked a long weapon case
and slid it beneath his seat.
   “Alice. Do you remember the route to the first stop?” Va-Elise asked.
   “My name is Alice, and I remember everything.”
   Jean-Charles laughed. “She’s been saving that one.”
   Alice clipped the map beside her wheel. “When the vehicles turn back, you stay
with us,” Sarah told her.
   Va-Elise drove the lead. Alice took the second, Jean-Charles beside her; Sarah,
Kailan and I were strapped into the row behind. Two more crews followed. The
sun cleared the horizon as all four swung onto the tar.
   The acceleration pinned the bag against my stomach. Road markers came at
us too fast to read. A rise lifted my weight out of the seat; the belt caught me and
the road struck back through the floor. Sarah gripped the handle above her head.
Jean-Charles watched the mirrors.
   The tar ended. Va-Elise left it at speed and a wall of dust rose behind her.
Alice drove along its edge, the front wheels hammering through ruts I saw as they
vanished beneath us. Once the lead vehicle came wholly clear of the ground. It
landed in a burst of sand and kept going. Through our rear glass the other two
appeared and disappeared in the enormous brown cloud rolling across the pan.
   A knot of vehicles stood far to the east. Men beside them turned to watch. I
had seen convoys like ours outside ministries; nobody came onto the track. Jean-
Charles kept his hand on the closed weapon case until they were gone behind the
dust.
   At a fork Alice followed Va-Elise’s single flash of brake light. We crossed the
wash so hard that my teeth met. Beyond it the track narrowed between rocks and
the engines dropped a note. I tried to ask how much farther. The next rut took
the words.




                                                                               243
Homo Noxius


   Near the end of the track stood one of Sarah’s stations, its door open to the
desert. Jean-Charles went in and called us after him. Sarah stopped with her hand
against the frame.
   “They had orders to keep this shut. Direct clearance for every visitor.”
   Three enamel cups stood beside a kettle. Water ran from the tap. Blood had
dried on the floor and the table lip; there were no bodies. A duty board bore three
names. On the back of a stores request someone had written door opened with.
The next letter was a downward stroke. I read the words into the recorder.
   Sarah tried the door with its key. The frame, hinges and lock were undamaged.
Va-Elise went out to examine the approach while Jean-Charles searched the rear
rooms. Beside the drum, a broken chair held the dark print of a hand.
   The drum kept turning. Five strokes came up from the ground beneath us
and drew themselves on its paper. Kailan stood before them without touching
the sheet. They were evenly spaced, with the same rise. When the fifth ended, the
stylus went back to its thin, irregular line. Sarah watched the interval and checked
her wristwatch.
   “Again in forty minutes,” she said. “These are the same five strokes. Same
height, same spacing. They are sounding on a programme.”
   A record box had burned in the corner. I lifted a blackened leaf with my forceps;
beneath it, fuel and water entries remained readable. The stores request reported
the same five-stroke signal. Va-Elise returned with photographs of tyre marks
beyond the rise. Outside the door she showed us two sets of prints and a scuff
that became parallel heel cuts.
   “Taken, probably,” Sarah said. “Three people who can read my notation and
show where these lines are buried. Elise, I want them back.”
   She read the names into my recorder with their offices and years of service. At
the third she looked back at the board. Va-Elise found the route drawer unlocked
and empty.
   “A visitor they admitted?” I asked.
   “Possibly. Or a key, or a threat. We have no witness.”
   She opened her station book and crossed six call signs. Beside them stood hours
and dead-drop times. Va-Elise copied the six entries.
   “They share outside support with this post,” Sarah said. “Recall each separately
through the central office, by its cleared channel. No resupply on these roads.
Bring out the people; break what they cannot carry. Nobody returns to Black-
wood.”



244
                                                                            Return


   She put the station key into Va-Elise’s palm. “Open the files before you burn
them.”
   Elise went out to the drivers. Sarah corrected the third man’s years of service
for my recorder. When we carried the water out, the next five strokes began. The
kettle answered against the table. Sarah counted the interval aloud and told us to
keep moving.


   The vehicles stopped where the track entered folded rock. A young man waited
there with two others and eight animals, his face bound against the glare. Sarah
called him Amastan.
   He counted us and our loads before he answered her. He rejected two water
tins whose caps rang against the handles and made me wrap my bag’s metal catches
in felt. His companions repacked the animals in the shade of the folds. When
nothing metal could strike metal, he uncovered his face.
   “The man you knew has been thirty years in the ground,” he said. “Ka-Dhavar
chose me to carry the name.” He drew the last strap through its ring. “Your officers
put it in their treaties long before either of us. They spelled it wrong.”
   Sarah told him about the emptied station and the missing three. He looked
toward the folds. “They searched the tents at the western well yesterday. My wife
and children are at the last hide. I take you to Shield if they come in with us.”
   “I cannot grant that,” Sarah said.
   Kailan came round the animal. “Sheva can hear you at the seam.”
   “Before any of you cross it.”
   “Yes.”
   Va-Elise opened a map on the bonnet of the lead vehicle. Amastan looked once,
drew a different line in the dust with the butt of his switch and put three stones
across the route she had marked.
   “That road was safe when I took over. It has tyre cuts now. Men watched it
two nights ago.” He moved the line west through a blank place on her map. “We
go here.”
   Amastan made Jean-Charles repeat the turns, corrected the third and erased
the drawing with his foot. Va-Elise gave Jean-Charles his tactical packet: this sector
and two abort points. “For the rest, ask him.”
   She collected the drivers’ maps and took the instruction-camera case back to
the lead vehicle. A spare driver from the fourth vehicle took Alice’s wheel. Alice
put her water and rolled prints into a saddlebag. Elise led the four vehicles out; be-
yond the folds two went north, one east and one toward the old tarmac. Amastan

                                                                                 245
Homo Noxius


waited until their dust had thinned, shifted Sarah’s saddle one hole and gave the
word to move.
    We set out at night and lay under ground-coloured cloth by day. Amastan
chose the hours, the wells and the order of drinking. The animals drank before
us.
    Amastan walked ahead without a light; his last companion brushed the places
where a hoof slipped. Jean-Charles twice offered a bearing. Amastan continued
along his own line. On the second night he raised a closed hand and every buckle
stilled. An engine reached us through the soles of my boots, became audible be-
yond the ridge and passed out of hearing before he lowered his hand.
    We reached a well before dawn, a crack at the back of a cleft. Amastan removed
six fitted slabs, drew water with a skin narrow enough to pass between them, wa-
tered the animals and replaced the slabs in reverse order. His companion brushed
the rock with a branch. When they had finished, I could not find the opening
from three paces away.
    “Does the Mountain keep this well?” I asked.
    “My people keep it.”
    Sarah rode in a sling they fitted around the saddle. At ninety-three she could
still mount on the first night. By the third, Amastan and Kailan had to lift her.
She allowed it without speaking. At noon her hand shook against the rim of her
cup. I set the machine between my knees and held the cup while she drank. When
it was empty she said, “Mr Morgan, if that appears in your account, at least spell
my name correctly.”
    “I always do.”
    “One useful quality.”
    Amastan shortened the next passage. He moved the longest climb into the
coldest hour and put Kailan at the left side of Sarah’s animal, where the ground
fell away. Sarah caught the guide’s sleeve before he could go ahead.
    “We have already lost half a day.”
    He looked down at her hand. “You could not lift your foot into the sling.”
    “I have crossed this country since before you were born.”
    “Then let him hold you on the climb.” Amastan waited. Sarah released his
sleeve and put her hand on the saddle.
    We left at the hour he had chosen. On the third night he stopped at a cairn
and counted its stones with his switch. “One has been added,” he said. He left it
standing and took us six hours around.



246
                                                                           Return


    At the next halt Kailan called the folds the Mountain’s outer wall, the last
country no state had crossed. Amastan had the end of a load strap between his
teeth. He drew it tight, fastened it and came round the animal to face Kailan.
    “Whose men changed that cairn?” he asked.
    Kailan looked back along the fold. “I do not know.”
    “Nor do I. That is why we walked around.” Amastan picked up his switch.
“Your Mountain may call this its wall. Ask it who keeps the wells.”
    Kailan moved back to the left of Sarah’s animal. I recorded speech in the
daylight hides and a few minutes at each change of ground: hoof-fall on sand,
iron striking buried stone. I marked the unrecorded intervals in my notebook.
Amastan held the ground-cloth over my hands while I changed batteries.
    On the fourth night the five strokes reached us without paper or instrument. I
felt the first through my feet. Sarah’s animal raised its head. Four more followed at
equal distances, too low to hear and distinct through the ground. Amastan put his
fingers to the rock. Sarah counted. We covered the metal fittings, gave the animals
the final water and went forward on foot.


    In the last dark of the fourth night we reached the high cleft. Amastan’s wife
waited under a cloth with two children. Two more animals stood tethered behind
her; filled water skins lay beside their packs. She spoke to Kailan in the Mountain’s
language. He asked which quarter she had come from.
    “We are of your people,” she said. “Both born outside, among the Kel Ad. We
trained in Dhavar’s rooms. These two have never been under stone.” The smaller
child held the edge of her sleeve.
    Amastan told her what Kailan had agreed. “The seam is below their posts,” he
said.
    “I have water for two nights. After that I must take them past the western well.”
She pulled a small bag from beneath the cloth. “I am coming with you.”
    His companions took charge of the animals. His wife stayed with the children
while Amastan brought us up through a dry wash to the camp overlook and
stopped us below its rim. He pressed a palm to the ground.
    “From there onward, their instruments can hear a stone roll.”
    He took Jean-Charles forward on his belly. When they returned, Jean-Charles
asked him to repeat the passage between two listening posts. Amastan traced the
first turns against the rock.
    “At the last bend you go alone. If they see me, they will watch the way I came.”



                                                                                247
Homo Noxius


   Jean-Charles removed his coat, covered the pale skin of his hands and face, and
tightened the cloth at his collar. He saw me watching.
   “Still taking notes on me, professor?”
   “Your questions at the safe house are on the record. So is your request that
Kailan keep them from Elise.”
   He pulled the cloth across his mouth. “And Sarah heard him tell it. She still
asked me to get her through their posts.”
   “That is on the record too.”
   “Good. Keep the machine dry.”
   Amastan led him over the rim and came back alone. I heard nothing from Jean-

   Grey light entered the pan while we lay against cold rock. Dishes, tents and
parked vehicles separated from the ground. Beyond them stood the drilling rig.
Two pale flecks crossed the sky without sound. Alice watched them, checked her
watch and gave Amastan the next times she expected overhead coverage. A dish
beside the command tents turned slowly and stopped. I kept my face below the
rim.
   Through the glass I saw men on the ridges facing inward, with tripods among
them pointed into the rock. Survey stakes made straight red lines across the pan.
Alice spread her latest print beneath the glass and marked two posts absent from it.
Sarah named a western truck’s position as the northern mouth of an old drovers’
track to the Oasis. Shield had allowed it to pass out of use, she said; no Outsider
was supposed to know where that mouth lay.
   “They were told,” Kailan said.
   “I think so.” Sarah pointed below the rig. “West of your Hall. What lies deeper
along that line?”
   “The reserve.”
   “Water?”
   He nodded.
   A double ring of tents occupied the camp’s centre. Generator housings stood
beside it, and men were loading stretchers into long vehicles. I watched an orderly
carry a folded blanket past the inner cordon. Armed men at its entrance kept a
truck waiting while its driver showed a paper. I handed the glass back.
   Sarah kept her eye to it. “A good half of what you see was written in my hand.”
She followed the orderly until he went inside a tent.
   “Some part of me wanted the Mountain found. I wanted not to be the last one
holding it.” She lowered the glass into the space between us.

                                                                           Return


     We waited through the daylight under cloth Amastan weighted below the rim.
Whenever he brought Sarah water, she asked whether Jean-Charles had returned.
Alice kept the glass on the wash. Men changed at the posts; vehicles moved be-
tween the rig and stacked pipe. Heat shook their outlines. When the shadow
reached our ledge again, Sarah sat up without help.
     We heard no shot. Late in the day, Jean-Charles appeared in the wash with
a man bent in front of him. Amastan went down to meet them, placing their
steps as they climbed. The prisoner wore the camp’s grey clothes. His wrists were
bound; a gag held his mouth. Jean-Charles carried a flat hard case and a black field
radio.
     “Post nine,” he said. “The seam is open beyond it. Not for long.”
     He set the case beside my recorder and said he had taken it from a locked drawer
in the command tent. I had not seen him enter it. Sarah opened the case with
the lid toward the camp, shielding the white paper. Consecutive issue marks ran
through the leaves; corrections impressed the sheets beneath them. I matched two
impressions against the light.
     “The leaves were together when these corrections were made,” I said. “I cannot
certify their contents.”
     Sarah touched the mark on the first sheet. “French strategic service. The min-
eral company is a cover. This is a state operation.”
     She translated into the Mountain’s language: bore depths and dates, capture
medicine, water control, approach denial. The personnel annex counted three
thousand and eighty-four people, including three hundred and eighty-six French
service numbers. I checked the subtotals.
     “The Algerian concessions are genuine,” she said. “But the central coordinates
are withheld from their copies. Each office can show its permits without seeing
what we have here.”
     Kailan leaned across the case. “What do they know of the mechanism?”
     “That there is one. These are orders to reach it. My grid, my methods.” She
held the page at its lower corners. “They have not written down what they expect
it to do.”
     Three leaves into the logistics annex, I found the local name Sarah had used for
the northern track. It stood among forty routes. Sarah translated the entry beside
it: source: internal, reliability high.
     “After Martinez was shut out, who held that corridor?”
     “One channel.” She kept the page on the rock between us.
     “Jean-Charles?”

   “Yes.”
   “The entry does not name him. And he brought us the case.”

He had been told there were “primitives” underground, a lost tribe to be handled
without loss of life. Sappers were to preserve what they found intact.

   “The same officer who sets the charges?”
   “No.”
   “Who can stop the digging?”

   “Who among you may speak to the men on the western ridge?”

of gravel and uncovered a narrow bronze plate with five shallow cups. He pressed

Homo Noxius


    “Bind it while she speaks,” Va-Sheva told the warden.

    “Yes. They have challenged us. The seam was seen.”
    “I have worked beside you. I trust your hands.” She sent the inner warden to
summon Shield and Justice.
    “Under necessity of Shield, I admit the four of you as far as the sealed floor
and no farther. Justice will decide whether the five hear you. The seam you used
is dead from this hour. I answer for the admission.”
    The warden drove a bronze pin into the five-cup plate. Three descending blows
sounded inside the wall. Va-Sheva called him away from the closed seam. “Take
the mother and children to the lower ward. Bring Amastan to Ka-Dhavar. He will
want the roads before he goes to Justice.”
    The warden returned her bag. She drew the older child’s hand back to her
belt and followed him. Amastan went with them. Va-Sheva lifted the lamp. Jean-
Charles and Kailan took Sarah between them; I followed through the inner door.
    At the crossings Va-Sheva sent wardens ahead for Stone’s survey and back for
the captured case. Sarah gave her the three bores, one deep and two shallow, and
the date the orders named for reaching the Hall.
    “I cannot certify their progress,” she said. “I need your survey. You may be able
to collapse a throat ahead of the bit, if you can lose the chambers beyond.”


254
                                                                         Return


   With a warden’s marking stone she drew the rig and its angle on the passage
wall. Kailan placed the Hall.
   “The reserve is deeper,” he said. “I cannot give you its depth.”
   Va-Sheva studied the marks and wiped them away with her sleeve. “Justice
will want what you saw, what she measured and what the papers say. Keep those
separate.”
   Where the stair turned, Sarah and Jean-Charles had gone several steps ahead.
Va-Sheva lowered her voice. I was one step behind Kailan with the recorder against
my ribs.
   “Elise wrote that you did not speak for eleven days at the house. Is it true?”
   “Yes.”
   “Could you drink?”
   “When she put the cup in my hands.”
   “Sleep?”
   “Not enough.”
   Va-Sheva raised the lamp to see the next step. “I would have ordered the con-
tainment again.”
   Kailan stayed below her. She lowered the lamp a little.
   “Do not ask me for more tonight,” she said. “I need to keep you alive long
enough to answer them.”
   She climbed after the others. Kailan waited until the space of one step had
opened between them, then followed.


   The route crossed an open stair above the common wards. Lamps burned over
food stalls. A woman walked twelve children in pairs; the smallest held the back
of the child’s tunic ahead of him and dragged one sandal. A seller weighed roots
in a hanging pan while two men disputed his weights. Someone beat dust from a
mat over a rail and earned a shout from the level beneath. Water ran in a channel
cut through the centre of the floor.
   No one looked upward as we came onto the stair. I had spent two years compar-
ing tool marks, copied signs and the mineral residue on objects Sarah put before
me. Below us a man mended a pot with wire. He held its broken halves between
his knees and worked the edges together until the painted band beneath the rim
joined across the break. He passed the wire through a drilled hole, drew it tight
and turned the pot to reach the other side. His cup stood outside the circle of
fragments at his feet. I wanted to ask what he used to seal the holes.


                                                                             255
Homo Noxius


    At a table under three lamps, a baker pressed his thumb into rounds of dough,
turned each one and pressed again. The evening bread reached us before we
crossed the centre of the stair: yeast, warm grain and the scorched flour at the edge
of an oven. I knew charred cakes from sites nine thousand years old. I knew the
pits in which the grain had been stored, the querns on which it had been broken
and the changes in human bone after people began to eat it every day. The baker
struck flour from his hands and called to a child to carry six loaves home.
    The recorder knocked against the rail. Several faces lifted. Va-Sheva caught my
sleeve before it struck again.
    “They do not know,” she said. “They will not learn it from you on a stair.
Walk.”
    The child with the six loaves had stopped beneath us. One loaf slipped against
his chest; he trapped it with his chin and laughed. I drew the machine close under
my coat and walked. At the upper turn the stalls disappeared. The smell remained
for twelve steps more.


   The first blow took the stair sideways.
   Stone struck my shoulder. The recorder drove into my ribs beneath my coat. A
lamp went out below; another rolled in a yellow circle until a hand caught it. Then
the sound arrived through the floor: one flat concussion, a single force driven into
the Mountain without the patient interval of the five-stroke sounding. Dust fell
from the joints overhead. Far inside the rock, worked stone ground against worked
stone.
   Sarah put both hands on the wall. “A charge. Down a shallow bore, close
enough to break a void or test what breaks around it. North-west, near the surface.
What lies there?”
   Va-Sheva had turned toward the sound. She stood with the lamp held clear of
the wall.
   “What lies there?” Sarah repeated.
   “Faith,” Va-Sheva said. “A Faith passage. The road to the Hall.”
   She ran. Jean-Charles went with her and the nearest warden. Sarah called for a
lamp, a plan of the north-western arc and the keeper of Stone. The ward children
below had begun to cry. The seller’s hanging scale knocked against its post with
each movement underfoot.

                                                                          Return


   Kailan put his palm to the wall. I put mine beside it. A tremor travelled through

passage to be cleared.
   I lifted the bag toward the grinding and felt the recorder’s motor through its
flap. A warden set his lamp beside Kailan and ran ahead. Kailan took it. We ran
after the others.

   (Paul): — Fresh reel, Kailan. We have reached the Mountain.

    I took the account back where the Justice road began. At my trial Ka-Elun had
led me through Faith’s Labyrinth. This time we came through Justice’s quarter.
Va-Sheva brought Sarah, Paul and me to a low door dressed as an ordinary joint
in the stone and struck it once with the heel of her hand. Ka-Leth opened it from

                                           259
Homo Noxius


within. Sa-Tavan stood behind him with a slate and a covered tablet of wet clay.
Another charge struck along the Faith road while Ka-Leth looked at the three of
us. Dust ran from the joint above the door and lay white across his shoulders.
    “They enter under my hand,” he said. “If the rooms refuse them, the hearing
does not happen.”
    Before the five could hear us, Justice had to admit us. There were five rooms
between that outer door and Justice’s door into the circular vestibule, each no
larger than a cell and each holding one problem cut into its stone. I had not known
they existed. Ka-Leth bound a folded cloth over Sa-Tavan’s eyes, took his sleeve
and led him through. At each latch the Keeper broke the red wax himself.
    The first was the balance of scale. Blocks of unequal size lay carved against the
weights they carried, and a beam leaned false across the wall. One loose stone
weight stood on the floor. Ka-Leth reckoned what each block was worth, ignored
its apparent size, and set the weight into the one notch that brought the beam
level. The first door released us deeper into the Justice road.
    In the second room, the nearest truth, the wall gave a sequence:


                   1 1 -1; 3 2 1; 7 5 -1; 17 12 1; 41 29 -1; 99 70 1.


    A square and its diagonal were cut beneath it. Ka-Leth entered two hundred
and thirty-nine over one hundred and sixty-nine. The second door gave before the
next strike reached us.
    The third was the false witness: one wolf among a thousand animals; a test that
named the wolf nine times in ten and falsely marked three innocent sheep in a
hundred; the true weight of guilt upon a sheep it marked. Ka-Leth set ten over
three hundred and forty-three.
    “That is barely three in a hundred,” Paul said.
    “A true mark is rare because the wolf is rare,” Sarah said. “The innocent marks
overwhelm it.”
    Ka-Leth kept his eyes on the door. “Justice asks what a sign proves, not how
frightening the sign appears.” The stone withdrew.
    The fourth, the reconciled record, gave four clerks’ remainders: counted by sev-
ens, six left; by elevens, eight; by thirteens, three; by seventeens, five; and the whole
below seventeen thousand and seventeen. Ka-Leth took the slate from Sa-Tavan
and worked that one while the Mountain took three more blows. Dust loosened
from the lintel. At the second blow Va-Sheva looked back toward Faith. She did
not ask him to hurry. He tested the number against all four clerks, entered four

                                                                             Seal


thousand nine hundred and sixty-nine, and the bolts went back. He wiped the

    At the bridge Ka-Leth removed Sa-Tavan’s blindfold. Behind us, the five doors

almost every other life in the world should be spent. The great Gate of the Hall
remained shut behind their seats. The ruling belonged in the antechamber.

   (Paul): — I was looking at the construction.
   (Kailan): — You were showing me around, Paul.
   (Paul): — I think you might allow me some enthusiasm.

offices that had tried me. Paul and Sarah stood unbound beside me, which was
not law, and was Va-Sheva’s doing. Amastan stood inside Shield’s arch beside
Jean-Charles and the wardens.
    Five human beings sat isolated in a dark large enough to lose a shout. The
token-flame that Faith keeps threw their shadows across the gravure. The shadows
shifted whenever the Mountain was struck, a deep flat knock coming up through
the floor into the soles of my bound feet.
    The five were older than the last time I had stood before them, and one I had
helped to make. Ka-Raedin of Light sat under the dark aperture that is Light’s,
my Keeper, the man whose ascension I had watched from beneath the pillar, in

                                                                              261
Homo Noxius


whose office I had served until I pulled the cord I was told to leave. He kept his
